x_{0}
reaches the boundary of
B_{Q}(R)
either without ever visiting the boundary of
B_{O}(R)
(which happens with probability at most
\mathbb{P}_{r_{0}}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}<T_{R}))
or after visiting the
said boundary for the first time (in which case the time to reach the boundary of
B_{Q}(R)
is dominated by the time of a particle starting at the boundary of
B_{O}(R)
and having
the same angular coordinate as the point
x_{0}
).
Thus,

\mathbb{P}_{r_{0}}(T_{\det}\leq\mathfrak{z})\leq\mathbb{P}_{r_{0}}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}<T_{R})+\mathbb{P}_{R}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}\leq\mathfrak{z}).

We will show that each of the right hand side terms above is
O(\delta)

where
\delta:=\delta(\kappa,\mathfrak{z})
. This immediately implies
the first part of the proposition’s statement.

By our choice of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}

and since
r_{0}\geq\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{r_{0}}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}<T_{R})\leq\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}<T_{R})=\delta.

By Part (
i
) of Lemma
14
with
y:=r
,
{\bf y}_{0}:=\mathfrak{r}_{0}
and
Y:=R
, and Markov’s inequality,
for
\lambda>0
,
\lambda_{1}:=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4}+2\lambda}+\frac{\alpha}{2}>0
and
\lambda_{2}:=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4}+2\lambda}-\frac{\alpha}{2}
, we get that

\mathbb{P}_{r_{0}}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\mathbb{P}_{r_{0}}(e^{-\lambda T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}}\geq e^{-\lambda\mathfrak{z}})\leq\frac{\lambda_{1}e^{-\lambda_{2}(R-r_{0})}+\lambda_{2}e^{\lambda_{1}(R-r_{0})}}{\lambda_{2}e^{\lambda_{1}(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}}e^{\lambda\mathfrak{z}}.

(15)

Observing that
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}=2\lambda
, that
\lambda_{2}>0
and
\lambda_{1}>\alpha
,
and taking
\lambda=\frac{1}{1+\mathfrak{z}}<1
(since by hypothesis
\mathfrak{z}>0
) it follows that

\mathbb{P}_{R}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}\leq\mathfrak{z})\leq\frac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{2}e^{\lambda_{1}(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}}\cdot e^{\lambda\mathfrak{z}}=O\Big(\frac{e^{\lambda\mathfrak{z}}}{\lambda}e^{-\lambda_{1}(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}\Big)=O((1+\mathfrak{z})e^{-\alpha(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}).

(16)

By our choice of
\delta
, we have
1+\mathfrak{z}\leq\delta(1+\kappa\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})^{2\alpha}=\Theta(\delta e^{\alpha R}(\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{2\alpha})
.
Moreover, by Part (
v
) of Lemma
8
, we have

|\theta_{0}|=\phi(\mathfrak{r}_{0})=\Theta(e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{r}_{0}})
.
Since by hypothesis
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, we know that
|\theta_{0}|\geq\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
,
it follows that
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{r}_{0}}=\Omega(\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
.
Thus,

\mathbb{P}_{R}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}\leq\mathfrak{z})=O\big(\delta(\phi(R)-\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{2\alpha}e^{\alpha\mathfrak{r}_{0}}\big)=O(\delta)

which establishes the first part of our stated result.

Next, we consider the second stated claim (the integral bound).
By (
15
), denoting by
\mu_{\theta_{0}}
the measure induced by
\mu
for the angle
\theta_{0}
, recalling that
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}=\alpha
, it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}}^{R}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu_{\theta_{0}}(r_{0})

\displaystyle=O(n)\int_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}}^{R}\frac{\lambda_{1}e^{-\lambda_{2}(R-r_{0})}+\lambda_{2}e^{\lambda_{1}(R-r_{0})}}{{\lambda_{2}e^{\lambda_{1}(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}}}\cdot e^{\lambda\mathfrak{z}}\cdot e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}

\displaystyle=O(n)\frac{e^{\lambda_{2}(R-\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0})}-e^{-\lambda_{1}(R-\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0})}}{{\lambda_{2}e^{\lambda_{1}(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}}}\cdot e^{\lambda\mathfrak{z}}

\displaystyle=O(n)\frac{e^{\lambda_{2}(R-\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0})}}{\lambda_{2}e^{\lambda_{1}(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}}\cdot e^{\lambda\mathfrak{z}}.

Using once more that
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}=\alpha
and
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}=2\lambda
, taking again
\lambda=\frac{1}{1+\mathfrak{z}}<1
,
we obtain

\int_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}}^{R}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu_{\theta_{0}}(r_{0})=O(n(1+\mathfrak{z})e^{-\alpha(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}e^{-\lambda_{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}).

Therefore, since
\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}\geq\mathfrak{r}_{0}
and
\lambda_{2}>0
imply that

\lambda_{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}-\mathfrak{r}_{0})
is positive, it follows that

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})=O(n(1+\mathfrak{z})e^{-\alpha R})\int_{\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e^{\alpha\mathfrak{r}_{0}}d\theta_{0}.

Using that
|\theta_{0}|=\phi(\mathfrak{r}_{0})=\Theta(e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{r}_{0}})
, since

\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
, by definition of
\delta
we get

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})=O(n(1+\mathfrak{z})e^{-\alpha R})\int_{\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\theta_{0}^{-2\alpha}d\theta_{0}=O(n\delta(\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})).

Since
\delta\leq 1
, the claimed integral bound follows from Lemma
18
.
∎

4.2
Uniform lower bound

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following lower bound matching the upper bound of Proposition
19
under the assumption that at least a constant amount of time has passed since the process started (as stated in Theorem
15
).

Proposition 20
.

For every fixed arbitrarily small constant
c>0

there are large enough constants
\kappa_{0},C\geq 1
such that
if
\kappa\geq\kappa_{0}
and
\mathfrak{z}\geq\frac{16}{\alpha}\log\kappa+C
satisfy
\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}-c
, then

\inf_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}(\kappa)}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\Omega(\kappa^{-2\alpha}).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}:=\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}(\kappa)
.
To obtain the lower bound on the detection probability recall that any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
either satisfies
|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
or
r_{0}\leq\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}
where
\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}
by definition is such that
G_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0})=\delta(\kappa,\mathfrak{z})
. We deal first with the case
\mathfrak{r}_{0}<r_{0}\leq\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}
(the case
r_{0}\leq\mathfrak{r}_{0}
is trivial since it implies that
x_{0}\in B_{Q}(R)
and thus
T_{det}=0
) by noticing that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})

\displaystyle\geq\,\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}\leq\mathfrak{z}\,\big|\,T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}<T_{R})\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}<T_{R})

\displaystyle\geq\,\big(1-\tfrac{1}{\mathfrak{z}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}\,\big|\,T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}<T_{R})\big)\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}<T_{R}).

Using Part (
iv
) of Lemma
14
with
y:=r
,
{\bf y}_{0}:=\mathfrak{r}_{0}
and
Y:=R
, recalling that
G_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}(r)=\mathbb{P}_{r}(T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}<T_{R})
, since by case assumption
r_{0}\leq\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}
and recalling again that by definition of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}
it holds that
G_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0})=\delta:=\delta(\kappa,\mathfrak{z})
, we get

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z}\big)\geq\Big(1-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{z}}\Big[\frac{2}{\alpha}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}-\mathfrak{r}_{0})+\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}(1-\delta)\Big]\Big)\delta.

From Fact
17
it follows that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}-\mathfrak{r}_{0}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\tfrac{1}{\delta}+O(1)
, and since we can bound from above
1-\delta
by
\log\tfrac{1}{\delta}
, we get that the term inside the brackets above is at most
\frac{4}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}+O(1)
. By hypothesis,
\mathfrak{z}\geq C\geq 1
, so
\delta\geq(1+\kappa)^{-2\alpha}=\Omega(\kappa^{-2\alpha})
and hence, using again the hypothesis regarding
\mathfrak{z}
, we see that
\mathfrak{z}>\frac{16}{\alpha}\log\kappa+C\geq\frac{8}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\frac{1}{2}C
for
C
large enough, so taking
C
even larger if needed we conclude that

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z}\big)=\Omega(\delta)=\Omega(\kappa^{-2\alpha}).

We now handle the points
x_{0}:=(r_{0},\theta_{0})\in\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
for which
|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
. Observe that for any fixed
\theta_{0}
the detection probability is minimized when
r_{0}=R
, and for points such that
r_{0}=R
the probability decreases with
|\theta_{0}|
, so it will be enough to consider the case where
r_{0}=R
and
|\theta_{0}|=\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
. To obtain lower bounds on the detection probability we will couple the radial movement
r_{t}
of the particle starting at
x_{0}
with a similar one, denoted by
\widetilde{r}_{t}
, evolving as follows:

•

\widetilde{r}_{0}=R
and we let
\widetilde{r}_{t}
evolve through the generator
\Delta_{rad}
on
(0,R+1]
.

•

Every time
\widetilde{r}_{t}
hits
R+1
it is immediately restarted at
R
.

It follows that
r_{t}
and
\widetilde{r}_{t}
can be coupled so that
r_{t}\leq\widetilde{r}_{t}
for every
t\geq 0
, and hence the detection time
\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}
of the new process bounds the original
T_{det}=T_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}
from above. Thus, it will be enough to bound
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}\leq\mathfrak{z})
from below. Observe that the trajectories of
\widetilde{r}_{t}
are naturally divided into excursions from
R
to
R+1
, which we use to define a sequence of Bernoulli random variables
\{E_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
where
E_{i}=1
if
\widetilde{r_{t}}
reaches
\mathfrak{r}_{0}
on the
i
-th excursion. We also use this division to define a sequence
\{\tau_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of random variables, where
\tau_{i}
is the time it takes
\widetilde{r}_{t}
to reach either
\mathfrak{r}_{0}
or
R+1
in the
i
-th excursion. It follows from the strong Markov property that all excursions are independent of one another, and so are the
E_{i}
’s and
\tau_{i}
’s.

Fix
m:=\lfloor\tfrac{\alpha}{24}\mathfrak{z}\rfloor
which from our hypothesis on
\mathfrak{z}
satisfies
m\geq 1
, and observe that, since the
E_{i}
’s are i.i.d., the event
\mathcal{E}:=\{\exists 1\leq i\leq m,\,E_{i}=1\}
has probability

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})=1-(1-\mathbb{P}(E_{1}))^{m}\geq 1-e^{-m\mathbb{P}(E_{1})}.

From Part (
iii
) of Lemma
14
with
y:=R
,
{\bf y}_{0}:=\mathfrak{r}_{0}
and
Y:=R+1
, using
\log(1+x)\geq\frac{x}{2}
for
|x|<1

and
\log(\cotanh(\frac{x}{2}))\leq\frac{2e^{-x}}{1-e^{-x}}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(E_{1})=\frac{\log\big(\tfrac{\tanh(\alpha(R+1)/2)}{\tanh(\alpha R/2)}\big)}{\log\big(\tfrac{\tanh(\alpha R/2)}{\tanh(\alpha\mathfrak{r}_{0}/2)}\big)}\geq\frac{\log\big(1+\frac{2e^{-\alpha R}(1-e^{-\alpha})}{(1+e^{-\alpha(R+1)})(1-e^{-\alpha R})}\big)}{\log\big(\cotanh(\alpha\frac{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}{2})\big)}=\Omega(e^{-\alpha(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}(1-e^{-\alpha\mathfrak{r}_{0}})).

Since
\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}-c
, we get
\mathfrak{r}_{0}=\Omega(1)
and deduce that
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})=\Omega(e^{-\alpha(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})})
.
Recalling that
\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=|\theta_{0}|=\phi(\mathfrak{r}_{0})
, by Part (
v
) of Lemma
8
, we have that

e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{r}_{0}}=\Theta(\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
and
\phi(R)=\Theta(e^{-\frac{1}{2}R})
.
Since by hypothesis
\mathfrak{z},\kappa\geq 1
, it follows that

e^{\frac{1}{2}(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}=\Theta(e^{\frac{1}{2}R}(\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(s)}))=\Theta(\kappa\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})
.
Thus,
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})=\Omega(\kappa^{-2\alpha}/\mathfrak{z})
so setting
\kappa_{0}
sufficiently large we get

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\geq 1-e^{-\Omega(\kappa^{-2\alpha})}=\Omega(\kappa^{-2\alpha}).

Clearly, if
i_{D}
is the first index such that
E_{i_{D}}=1
, then
\mathcal{E}=\{1\leq i_{D}\leq m\}
. Hence,

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})\geq\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z}\mid\mathcal{E})\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\mathcal{E})=\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{i_{D}}\tau_{i}\leq\mathfrak{z}\mid 1\leq i_{D}\leq m\Big)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}),

where the
\tau_{i}
were defined above. Using Markov’s inequality we obtain

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{i_{D}}\tau_{i}>\mathfrak{z}\,\big|\,1\leq i_{D}\leq m\Big)\leq\frac{1}{\mathfrak{z}}\big(m\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}(\tau_{1}\mid E_{1}=0)+\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}(\tau_{1}\mid E_{1}=1)\big),

since there are at most
m
excursions where the particle fails to reach
\mathfrak{r}_{0}
, followed by a single excursion where it succeeds (here we are conditioning on these events through the value of
E_{1}
). For the first term, we have

\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}(\tau_{1}\mid E_{1}=0)=\mathbb{E}_{R}(\widetilde{T}_{R+1}\mid\widetilde{T}_{R+1}\leq\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}})\,\leq\,\frac{6}{\alpha},

as can be seen in
[
11
]
(formulas 3.0.4 and 3.0.6). by comparing the process to one with constant drift equal to
\tfrac{\alpha}{2}
. For the second term we use Part (
iv
) of Lemma
14
with
y:=R
,
{\bf y}_{0}:=\mathfrak{r}_{0}
and
Y:=R+1
to obtain

\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}(\tau_{1}\mid E_{1}=1)=\mathbb{E}_{R}(\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}\mid\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}<\widetilde{T}_{R+1})\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}(R+1-\mathfrak{r}_{0})+\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}.

Recalling that

e^{\frac{1}{2}(R-\mathfrak{r}_{0})}=\Theta(\kappa\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})
,
we have
R-\mathfrak{r}_{0}=\log(\kappa\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})+\Theta(1)
.
Summarizing,

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{i_{D}}\tau_{i}>\mathfrak{z}\mid 1\leq i_{D}\leq m\Big)=\frac{6m}{\alpha\mathfrak{z}}+\frac{1}{\mathfrak{z}}\Big(\frac{2}{\alpha}\log(\kappa\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})+O(1)\Big).

By our choice of
m
it is immediate that
\frac{6m}{\alpha\mathfrak{z}}\leq\frac{1}{4}
.
To bound the last term of the displayed equation recall that by hypothesis
\mathfrak{z}\geq\frac{16}{\alpha}\log\kappa+C
,
so that we have
\frac{2}{\alpha}\log\kappa\leq\frac{1}{8}\mathfrak{z}
and moreover we can choose

C
large enough so that
\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\log\mathfrak{z}\leq\frac{1}{8}\mathfrak{z}
and finally conclude that

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\Big(1-\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{i_{D}}\tau_{i}>\mathfrak{z}\,\big|\,1\leq i_{D}\leq m\Big)\Big)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})=\Omega(\kappa^{-2\alpha}).

∎

Observe that the bounds established in the preceding proposition are exactly those claimed in the first part of Theorem
15
.

4.3
Time spent within an interval containing the origin

In this section we consider a class of one-dimensional diffusion processes taking place in
[0,Y]
with
Y
a positive integer and with given stationary distribution.
Our study is motivated by the process
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
in
(0,Y]
with generator
\Delta_{h}

(see (
4
)) and reflecting barrier at
Y
. This section’s main result
will later in the paper be applied precisely to this latter process. However, the arguments of this section are applicable to less constrained diffusion processes and might even be further
generalized to a larger class of processes, and so we have opted for an exposition which, instead
of tailoring the proof arguments to the specific
process with generator
\Delta_{h}
, gives the conditions that need to be satisfied so that
our results as a corollary also apply to our specific process.

Our goal is to formalize a rather intuitive fact. Specifically, assume

\mathfrak{z}
is not a too small amount of time and that

\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
is a diffusion process with stationary distribution
\pi(\cdot)
on
[0,Y]
. Roughly speaking, we show that under some general hypotheses, during a time interval of length
\mathfrak{z}
, when starting from the stationary distribution, with constant probability the process spends within
(0,k]
(
k
larger than a constant) a period of time proportional to the expected time to spend there, that is, proportional to
\mathfrak{z}\pi((0,k])
. We remark that
k
only needs to be larger than a sufficiently large constant
C
, which does not depend on
Y
(otherwise the result would follow directly from Markov’s inequality), and
k
does not depend on
\pi
either.

We start by establishing two lemmas. The first one states that a diffusion process
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
in
[0,Y]
with stationary distribution
\pi(\cdot)
,
henceforth referred to as the
original process
, can be coupled with a process
\mathfrak{D}
that is continuous in time but discretized in space with values in
\mathbb{Z}
and defined next.
First, let
\pi_{m}:=\pi((m-1,m])
where
1\leq m\leq Y
.
Let
p^{\prime}_{1}=0
(this restriction could be relaxed, but we impose it for the sake of simplicity of presentation), and let
p^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{Y}
be the unique solution to the following system of equations:

\pi_{m}=\begin{cases}p^{\prime}_{m}\pi_{m}+p^{\prime}_{m+1}\pi_{m+1},&\text{if $m=1$,}\\
(1-p^{\prime}_{m-1})\pi_{m-1}+p^{\prime}_{m+1}\pi_{m+1},&\text{if $1<m<Y$,}\\
(1-p^{\prime}_{m-1})\pi_{m-1}+(1-p^{\prime}_{m})\pi_{m},&\text{if $m=Y$.}\end{cases}

(17)

Intuitively, one can think of the
p^{\prime}_{m}
as the probability that the process
hits
m-1
before it hits
m
when starting from the stationary distribution conditioned on starting in the interval
(m-1,m]
. Denoting next, for
0\leq m<Y
, by

\widehat{p}_{m}
the probability that the original process starting at
m
hits
m-1
before hitting
m+1
(observe that
\widehat{p}_{0}=0
). We then define

p:=\sup\{\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}:1\leq m\leq Y}p^{\prime}_{m},\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}:0\leq m<Y}\widehat{p}_{m}\},

and let
q:=1-p
.
We are exclusively interested in processes for which the following holds:

(H1)
p\in[\underline{c},\overline{c}]
,
0<\underline{c}\leq\overline{c}<1/2
,
for constants
\underline{c},\overline{c}>0

that do not depend on
Y
.

In other words, we are interested in the case
where the original process is subject to a drift towards the boundary
Y
.

Note however that condition
(H1)
does not preclude that the process
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
, being at position
y
(for some real
0\leq y\leq Y
), in expectation stays within an interval
(y-1,y+1]\cap[0,Y]
an amount of time that increases with
Y
. This explains why we focus on processes that satisfy the next stated additional hypothesis concerning the maximal time
T_{y\pm 1}
the process stays within said interval:

(H2)
There is a constant
L>0
(independent of
Y
) such that
for all reals
0\leq y\leq Y
it holds that

\mathbb{E}_{y}(T_{y\pm 1})\leq L
.

The preceding hypothesis says that the process stays, when starting at position
y
, in expectation, at most constant time within any giving interval
(y-1,y+1]
.
A last assumption that we make in order to derive this section’s main result is the following:

(H3)
There is a constant

\widetilde{C}
, independent of
Y
, such that if
\widetilde{C}\leq k\leq Y
,
\mathfrak{z}\geq 1/\pi((0,k])
and

y_{s}\leq k
at some time moment
s\geq 0
, then with constant probability
y_{t}\leq k
for all
s\leq t\leq s+1
.

The preceding hypothesis says that only in the vicinity of
0
there might exist an infinite drift (towards
Y
). Once we reach a point that is a constant away from
0
, we might stay at roughly this value for at least one unit of time with constant probability (this is a technical assumption that allows us to focus only on the probability to reach such a value when considering the expected time spent roughly there.

Now, we are ready to define the process
\mathfrak{D}
: set the initial position of
\mathfrak{D}
as
y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}:=\lfloor y_{0}\rfloor
. Suppose the original process starts in an interval
(m-1,m]
for some integer
1\leq m\leq Y
. If the original process hits
m
before hitting
m-1
, the original process stays put at
m-1
and the
coupling phase
defined below starts. If the original process hits
m-1
before hitting
m
, then
\mathfrak{D}
jumps to
m-2
and the coupling phase starts as well. In the coupling phase now iteratively the following happens: suppose the original process is initially at integer
m
, for some
1\leq m<Y
, and
\mathfrak{D}
is at some integer
m^{\prime}
(if
m=Y
, directly go to the
move-independent phase
defined below). Mark
m
as the center of the current phase, and do the following: As long as the original process neither hits
m-1
nor
m+1
,
\mathfrak{D}
stays put. If the original process hits
m-1
, then
\mathfrak{D}
jumps to
m^{\prime}-1
(note that this happens with probability
\widehat{p}_{m}\leq p
. If the original process hits
m+1
, then
\mathfrak{D}
jumps to
m^{\prime}-1
with probability
\frac{p-\widehat{p}_{m}}{\widehat{q}_{m}},
and with probability
1-\frac{p-\widehat{p}_{m}}{\widehat{q}_{m}},

\mathfrak{D}
jumps to
m^{\prime}+1
. Note that the total probability that
\mathfrak{D}
jumps to
m^{\prime}-1
is exactly
p
. Moreover, if
m+1=Y
, the move-independent phase starts. In either case,
m
is unmarked as the center of the current phase, and the new location of the original process (either
m-1
or
m+1
, respectively; note that
m-1=0
could also happen, this is treated in the same way), is marked as the new center, and the next iteration of the coupling phase (move-independent phase, respectively) with this new location playing the role of
m
.

In the move-independent phase
only the instants
of movements of the original process and
\mathfrak{D}
are coupled, but not the movements itself: suppose that at the beginning of this phase the original process is at position
m
for some integer
0\leq m\leq Y
, whereas
\mathfrak{D}
is at some integer
m^{\prime}
: at the instant when the original process hits
m-1
or
m+1
,
\mathfrak{D}
jumps to
m^{\prime}-1
with probability
p
and to
m^{\prime}+1
with probability
q
. Then, the next iteration of the independent phase starts with the new location of the original process (that is, either
m-1
or
m+1
) playing the role of
m
.

By construction, it is clear that from the beginning of the coupling phase on,
\mathfrak{D}
jumps from
m^{\prime}
to
m^{\prime}-1
with probability
p
and from
m^{\prime}
to
m^{\prime}+1
with probability
1-p
. We also have the following observation that also follows immediately by construction:

Observation 21
.

As long as the original process
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
does not hit
Y
, at every time instant
s
,
y_{s}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq y_{s}
.

We next show that for
T
being a sufficiently large constant
C_{L}
that depends on
L
only, conditional under not yet having hit the boundary,
\mathfrak{D}
is likely to have performed already quite a few steps up to time
T
:

Lemma 22
.

Assume
(H2)
holds. Let
C_{L}
be a large enough constant depending on
L
only with
L
as in
(H2)
. For every constant
c_{1}
, there exists a constant
c_{2}>0
, depending only on
c_{1}
and
L
, such that for any integer
T\geq C_{L}
, with probability at least
1-e^{c_{1}T}
, at least
c_{2}T
steps are performed by
\mathfrak{D}
up to time
T
.

Proof.

Fix any real
0\leq y\leq Y
, and recall that
T_{y\pm 1}
denotes the time the original process remains in the interval
(y-1,y+1]\cap[0,Y]
. By
(H2)
and Markov’s inequality, with probability at most
1/2
, the original process exits
(y-1,y+1]
before time
2L
. Formally, for all
0\leq y\leq Y
, we have
\mathbb{P}_{y}(T_{y\pm 1}\geq 2L)\leq 1/2
.
Since this bound is uniform over the starting position
y
we can restart the process every
2L
units of time, thus obtaining

\mathbb{P}(T_{y\pm 1}\geq 2kL)\leq 2^{-k},

(18)

for every integer
k\geq 1
, regardless of
y
. Hence, if at the beginning of an iteration of the coupling phase the original process is at some integer
0\leq m\leq Y
, the time it takes
\mathfrak{D}
to wait until its next step from that time moment on, is bounded in the same way as
T_{m\pm 1}
, and the time for the first step to enter the coupling phase can be bounded in the same way as in (
18
). Now, for
i\geq 1
, let
T^{(i)}
denote the time spent between the instants
T_{i-1}
and
T_{i}
, that mark the beginning and end of the
i
-th iteration of the coupling phase (the time before the start of the first iteration of the coupling phase, in case
i=1
, respectively). By (
18
), the random variable
\lfloor\frac{T^{(i)}}{2L}\rfloor
is stochastically bounded from above by a geometric random variable with success probability
\frac{1}{2}
and support
\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
. The random variables
T^{(1)},T^{(2)},\ldots
are independent but not identically distributed, as the time to remain in some intervals might be longer than in others, but since (
18
) holds for all
y
, we may bound
\sum_{i}\lfloor\frac{T^{(i)}}{2L}\rfloor
by the sum of i.i.d. geometric random variables with success probability
\frac{1}{2}
. Hence, for any constant
c_{1}
, there is a sufficiently small
c_{2}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{c_{2}T-1}T^{(i)}>T\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{c_{2}T-1}\left\lfloor\frac{T^{(i)}}{2L}\right\rfloor>\frac{T}{3L}\Big)\leq\sum_{j=\lfloor\frac{T}{3L}\rfloor+1}^{\infty}\binom{j-1}{c_{2}T-1}2^{-j}\leq e^{-c_{1}T},

where we used that the sum of
c_{2}T
i.i.d. geometric random variables has a negative binomial distribution, and where
c_{2}
is chosen (depending on
c_{1}
and
L
only) so that the last inequality holds.
Hence, with probability at least
1-e^{c_{1}T}
, at least
c_{2}T
steps are performed by
\mathfrak{D}
during time
T
, and the lemma follows.
∎

From the previous lemma we have the following immediate corollary:

Corollary 23
.

Assume
(H2)
holds. Let
C_{L}
be a large enough constant depending on
L
only with
L
as in
(H2)
. Let
C^{\prime}_{L}
be a sufficiently large constant depending on
L
only and let
s\geq C^{\prime}_{L}
denote the number of steps performed by
\mathfrak{D}
. For every constant
c^{\prime}_{1}>0
, there exists
C^{\prime}_{1}
depending only on
L
and on
c^{\prime}_{1}
, such that with probability at least
1-e^{c^{\prime}_{1}s}
, the time elapsed for the
s
steps is at most
C^{\prime}_{1}s
.

When
p(\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0})<1/2
, the process
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
is subject to a drift towards the boundary
Y
,
so intuitively the process
\mathfrak{D}
will also hit
Y
in a number of steps
that is proportional to
Y-y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}
(the initial distance of the process
\mathfrak{D}
to the boundary

Y
) and dependent on the intensity of the drift. Moreover, one should expect that the probability of the said number of steps exceeding its expected value by much decreases rapidly. The next result is a quantitative version of this intuition.

Lemma 24
.

Let
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
be a diffusion process on
[0,Y]
and
suppose that
\mathfrak{D}
is such that
y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}\geq m
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
p:=p(\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0})
and assume
(H1)
holds. Then, for any
0<\delta<\frac{1}{2p}-1
and all
C\geq(1-2(1+\delta)p)^{-1}
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{2}p\tau)
, the process

\mathfrak{D}
hits
Y
in at most
\tau=\tau(m):=\lfloor C(Y-m)\rfloor
steps.

Proof.

Denote by
U
the random variable counting the number of time steps up to step
\tau
where
\mathfrak{D}
decreases its value (that is, jumps from some integer
m^{\prime}
to
m^{\prime}-1
).
If the process
\mathfrak{D}
does not hit
Y
during
\tau
steps, then it
must have decreased for
U
steps and increased during
\tau-U
steps
(so
y^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\tau}=\tau-2U+y^{\mathfrak{D}}_{0}
), and moreover
y^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\tau}

would have to be smaller than
Y
.
Thus, it will suffice to bound from above the probability that
\tau-2U=y^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\tau}-y^{\mathfrak{D}}_{0}<Y-m
.
Since
\mathbb{E}(U)=p\tau
and by Chernoff bounds (see
[
32
, Theorem 4.4(2)]
), for any
0<\delta<1
,

\mathbb{P}(U\geq(1+\delta)\mathbb{E}(U))\leq e^{-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{2}\mathbb{E}(U)},

the claim follows observing that
\frac{1}{2}(C-1)(Y-m)\geq(1+\delta)\mathbb{E}(U)
(by hypothesis on
\delta
and
C
) and
\tau-2U<Y-m
if and only if
U>\tfrac{1}{2}(C-1)(Y-m)
.
∎

Next, we show that, with high probability over the choice of the starting position, the boundary
Y
is hit quickly:

Lemma 25
.

Let
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
be a diffusion process on

[0,Y]
with
\pi(\cdot)
its stationary distribution.
Assume
(H1)
and
(H2)
hold.
Then, there is a
\widehat{C}>0
such that if
0<k\leq Y
, then
the original process has not hit
Y

by time
\widehat{C}\log(1/\pi((0,k]))
with probability at most
3\pi([0,k])
.

Proof.

Define the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}:=\{y_{0}\in(0,k]\}
and observe that

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\mathcal{E}_{1})=\pi((0,k]).

Next, fix
0<\delta\leq 1
,
p
as before, and let
C^{\prime}:=C^{\prime}(\delta)\geq 3/(\delta^{2}p)

be a constant which is at least as large as the
constant
C
of Lemma
24
(this last result is applicable because
(H1)
holds).
Define
\tau:=\lceil C^{\prime}\log(1/\pi((0,k]))\rceil
and
let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be the event that the process
\mathfrak{D}
does not hit
Y
during
\tau
steps.
Conditioned on
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
(so
y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}\geq k
since
y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}>y_{0}-1>k-1
), by our choice of
C^{\prime}
and Lemma
24
with
m:=k
,

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\mathcal{E}_{2}\mid\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{1})\leq\exp\big({-}\tfrac{1}{3}\delta^{2}p\tau\big)\leq\pi((0,k]).

Choosing
C^{\prime}
sufficiently large, by Corollary
23
, applied with
c^{\prime}_{1}:=1
, there exists
C^{\prime}_{1}
large enough, so that with probability at least
1-e^{-\tau}
, the time elapsed for
\tau
steps is at most
C^{\prime}_{1}\tau
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{3}
be the event that the original process has not hit
Y
by time

C^{\prime}_{1}\tau
. Since
\delta,p\leq 1
, by definition of
C^{\prime}
, we have
C^{\prime}>1
, so it follows by definition of
\tau
that
\tau\geq\log(1/\pi((0,k]))
, and thus our preceding discussion establishes that

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\mathcal{E}_{3}\mid\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{1},\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{2})\leq\pi((0,k]).

The lemma follows by a union bound over events
\mathcal{E}_{1}
,
\mathcal{E}_{2}
,
\mathcal{E}_{3}
, and by setting
\widehat{C}:=C^{\prime}C^{\prime}_{1}
.
∎

In order to establish our main result we next state the following fact:

Fact 26
.

For all integers
1<m<Y
,
q\pi_{m-1}\leq p\pi_{m}
.

Proof.

Let
p^{\prime}_{1},...,p^{\prime}_{Y}
be defined as in this subsection’s introduction and let
q^{\prime}_{i}:=1-p^{\prime}_{i}
, and recall that
p^{\prime}_{1}=0
by assumption.
We claim that
q^{\prime}_{m-1}\pi_{m-1}=p^{\prime}_{m}\pi_{m}
for
1<m\leq Y
.
Indeed, by (
17
), we have that
\pi_{1}=p^{\prime}_{1}\pi_{1}+p^{\prime}_{2}\pi_{2}
, so
q^{\prime}_{1}\pi_{1}=p^{\prime}_{2}\pi_{2}
. Assume the claim holds for
1\leq m<Y
. By (
17
) and the inductive hypothesis, we have
p^{\prime}_{m+1}\pi_{m+1}=\pi_{m}-q^{\prime}_{m-1}\pi_{m-1}=\pi_{m}-p^{\prime}_{m}\pi_{m}=q^{\prime}_{m}\pi_{m}
, which concludes the inductive proof of our claim.

By definition of
p
, we have
p^{\prime}_{m}\leq p
and
q^{\prime}_{m}\geq q
for all
1\leq m<Y
. Hence, by the preceding paragraph’s claim, for
1<m<Y
we get
q\pi_{m-1}\leq q^{\prime}_{m-1}\pi_{m-1}=p^{\prime}_{m}\pi_{m}\leq p\pi_{m}
, and the fact follows.
∎

We now use the previous lemmata to show this section’s main result:

Proposition 27
.

Let
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
be a diffusion process on
[0,Y]
with stationary distribution
\pi(\cdot)
. Assume
(H1)
,
(H2)
and
(H3)
hold.
Then, there are constants
\widetilde{C}>0
,
\widetilde{c}\in(0,1)
,
\widetilde{\eta}>0
all independent of
Y
such that for all

\widetilde{C}\leq k\leq Y
and
\mathfrak{z}\geq 1/\pi((0,k])
we have

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}\big(I_{k}\geq\widetilde{c}\mathfrak{z}\pi((0,k])\big)\geq\widetilde{\eta},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where $I_{k}:=\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{z}}{\bf 1}_{(0,k]}(y_{s})ds$.}

Proof.

First, we establish that there is an integer
\Delta>0
depending on
p
alone such that

\pi((0,k-\Delta])\leq\frac{1}{12}\pi((0,k])\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq k\leq Y$.}

(19)

The claim is a consequence of
(H1)
and Fact
26
:
Indeed, let
p,q
be as therein. For any
k
, summing
over
m
with
1<m\leq k
we get
q\pi((0,k-1])\leq p\pi((0,k])
and thus

\pi((0,k-m])\leq(p/q)^{m}\pi((0,k])
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
By
(H1)
we know that
p/q
is bounded away from
1
by a constant independent of
Y
.
Taking
m
large enough so that
(p/q)^{m}\leq 1/12
the claim follows setting
\Delta:=m
.

Next, we will split the time period
[0,\mathfrak{z}]
into time intervals of one unit (throwing away the eventual remaining non-integer part): for the
i
-th time interval let
X_{i}
be the indicator random variable being
1
if at time instant
i-1
(that is, at the beginning of the
i
-th time interval) the process
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
is within
(0,k]
, and
0
otherwise. Since
\pi
is stationary,
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{i}=1)=\pi((0,k])
for any
i
. Thus, setting
X:=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor\mathfrak{z}\rfloor}X_{i}
, we have
(\mathfrak{z}-1)\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1)<\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X)\leq\mathfrak{z}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1)\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X)
.
Since
(H3)
holds, for
\widetilde{C}
the constant therein, if
X_{i}=1
and
k\geq\widetilde{C}
, then with constant probability the process
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
stays throughout the entire
i
-th time interval within
(0,k]
.
So by our previous discussion, to establish the desired
result, it suffices to show that for some
\xi>0
the probability that
X
is smaller than
\xi\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X)
is at most a constant strictly less than
1
. We rely on Chebyshev’s inequality to do so and thus need to bound from above the variance of
X
. This explains why we now undertake the determination of an upper bound for
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X^{2})
.
Since there are at most
P_{d}\leq\mathfrak{z}
pairs of random variables
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
such that
|i-j|=d
,

\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X^{2})=\sum_{i,j}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{i}=1,X_{j}=1)\leq\sum_{d=0}^{\lfloor\mathfrak{z}\rfloor}P_{d}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1).

In order to bound
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1)
we construct two processes
\{\widetilde{y}_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
and
\{\widehat{y}_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
as follows: Initially,
\widetilde{y}_{0}
is sampled with
distribution
\frac{\pi(\cdot\cap(0,k])}{\pi((0,k])}
.
Also, with probability
\pi((0,k])
we let
\widehat{y}_{0}=\widetilde{y}_{0}
, and otherwise we sample
\widehat{y}_{0}
with distribution
\frac{\pi(\cdot\cap(k,Y])}{\pi((k,Y])}
.
Then, both processes evolve independently according to the same law as the original process until they first meet. Afterward, they still move as the original process but coupled together.
It follows directly that
\{\widehat{y}_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
is a copy of the original process, while
\{\widetilde{y}_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
is a version of the original process conditioned to start within
(0,k]
. Let
\widetilde{T}_{Y}
be the first time that
\{\widetilde{y}_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
hits
Y
, and define
\widetilde{X}_{i}
and
\widehat{X}_{i}
analogously to the variables
X_{i}
with
\widetilde{y}_{s}
and
\widehat{y}_{s}
playing the role of
y_{s}
, respectively. Since
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1)=\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{X}_{1+d}=1)\pi((0,k])
,
it follows that

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1)\,=\,\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{X}_{1+d}=1,\widetilde{T}_{Y}\leq d)\pi((0,k])+\mathbb{P}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1,\widetilde{T}_{Y}>d).

For the first term on the right-hand side above observe that
\widetilde{y}_{0}\leq\widehat{y}_{0}
, hence on the event
\widetilde{T}_{Y}\leq d
both processes have met before time
d
, and so by definition
\widetilde{X}_{1+d}=\widehat{X}_{1+d}
, yielding

\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{X}_{1+d}=1,\widetilde{T}_{Y}\leq d)\pi((0,k])\leq\mathbb{P}(\widehat{X}_{1+d}=1)\pi((0,k])=\big(\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1)\big)^{2}.

It remains then to bound the term
\mathbb{P}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1,\widetilde{T}_{Y}>d)
. To do so recall that
\{\widetilde{y}_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
is a version conditioned to start at
(0,k]
so calling
T_{Y}
the first time
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
hits
Y
, we have

\mathbb{P}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1,\widetilde{T}_{Y}>d)=\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1,T_{Y}>d).

First, recall that by Lemma
22
, if
d\geq C_{L}
for some large enough constant depending on
L
only, for every
c_{1}>0
there exists
c_{2}>0
, depending only on
c_{1}
and on
L
, so that the process
\mathfrak{D}
performs at least
c_{2}d
steps during the time interval
[0,d]
with probability at least
1-e^{-c_{1}d}
. Note that
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
do not depend on
\widetilde{C}
. Let
\mathcal{E}
be the event that
\mathfrak{D}
performed at least
c_{2}d
steps up to time
d
. Now,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1,T_{Y}>d)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1,T_{Y}>d,\mathcal{E})+\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1,\overline{\mathcal{E}})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1,T_{Y}>d,\mathcal{E})+e^{-c_{1}d}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1).

Next, recall that as long as the original process does not hit the boundary, by Observation
21
, the auxiliary process satisfies
y_{s}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq y_{s}
for every instant
s
. Formally,

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1,T_{Y}>d)\leq\mathbb{P}(y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k,y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k)=\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1)\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k),

and also

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1,X_{1+d}=1,T_{Y}>d,\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k,y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k,\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1)\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k\mid\mathcal{E},y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k),

and our goal is thus to bound
\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k\mid\mathcal{E},y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k)
.
Recalling that
p<1/2
(by hypothesis) is the probability that
\mathfrak{D}
makes a decreasing step, and
q:=1-p
, we have for some large enough constant
C_{1}>0

\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k,\mathcal{E})\leq\sum_{\ell:2\ell\geq c_{2}d}\binom{2\ell}{\ell}(pq)^{\ell}\leq\sum_{\ell:2\ell\geq c_{2}d}(4pq)^{\ell}\leq C_{1}(4pq)^{c_{2}d/2}.

The same upper bound holds for
\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k{-}1\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k,\mathcal{E})
. For
\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k{-}2\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k,\mathcal{E})
and
\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k{-}3\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k,\mathcal{E})
note that
\mathfrak{D}
has to make one more decreasing step and one less increasing step, yielding an upper bound of

C_{1}(4pq)^{c_{2}d/2}\frac{p}{q}
. In the same way,

\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k{-}2i\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k,\mathcal{E})\leq C_{1}(4pq)^{c_{2}d/2}(\frac{p}{q})^{i}
, so that for
C_{2}:=C_{2}(C_{1})>0
large enough,

\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k,\mathcal{E})\leq C_{2}(4pq)^{c_{2}d/2}
.
We can get the same upper bound also for
\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k{-}1,\mathcal{E})
.
Moreover,
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k{-}2,\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell:2\ell\geq c_{2}d}\binom{2\ell}{\ell-1}p^{\ell{-}1}q^{\ell+1}\leq\frac{q}{p}\sum_{\ell:2\ell\geq c_{2}d}\binom{2\ell}{\ell}(pq)^{\ell}\leq\frac{q}{p}C_{1}(4pq)^{\frac{c_{2}d}{2}}.

By the argument from before, we get

\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k{-}2,\mathcal{E})\leq(q/p)C_{2}(4pq)^{\frac{c_{2}d}{2}}

and iterating the argument, also obtain

\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k{-}2i,\mathcal{E})\leq(q/p)^{i}C_{2}(4pq)^{\frac{c_{2}d}{2}}.

Hence, if
k
is even,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k,y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k\mid\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k{-}j,\mathcal{E})\mathbb{P}(y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k{-}j)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=0,\mathcal{E})\mathbb{P}(y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=0)+\!\!\sum_{i=0}^{k/2-1}\mathbb{P}(y_{d}^{\mathfrak{D}}\leq k\mid y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}=k{-}2i,\mathcal{E})\mathbb{P}(y_{0}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\{k{-}2i,k{-}2i{-}1\})

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq(q/p)^{k/2}C_{2}(4pq)^{\frac{c_{2}d}{2}}\pi((0,1])+2\sum_{i=0}^{k/2-1}\Big(\frac{q}{p}\Big)^{i}C_{2}(4pq)^{\frac{c_{2}d}{2}}\pi((k{-}2i{-}2,k{-}2i])

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq 4C_{3}(4pq)^{\frac{c_{2}d}{2}}\pi((0,k]),

where for the secondlast inequality we used Fact
26
.
If
k
is odd, then take
\{0,...,\frac{1}{2}(k-1)\}
as the range over which the second index of the summation (the one over
i
above); the remaining bounds are still valid, and
we obtain the same bound as for
k
even.
Thus, recalling that
P_{d}\leq\mathfrak{z}
denotes the number of pairs of random variables
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
for which
|i-j|=d
, and recalling that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
holds with probability at most
e^{-c_{1}d}
(in which case the joint probability is only bounded by the probability of
X_{1}=1
), and adding also the joint probability
(\mathbb{P}(X_{1}=1))^{2}
in case
\widetilde{T}_{Y}\leq d
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{z}C_{L}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1)+\sum_{d\geq C_{L}}^{\lfloor\mathfrak{z}\rfloor}P_{d}\Big(\big(4C_{3}(4pq)^{\frac{c_{2}d}{2}}+e^{-c_{1}d}\big)\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1)+\big(\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1)\big)^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X)+2C^{*}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X)+(\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X))^{2}.

where for the second inequality we used that
P_{d}\leq\mathfrak{z}
and then that
\mathfrak{z}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{1}=1)\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X)
, and that
\sum_{d\geq 0}(4C_{3}(4p(1-p))^{c_{2}d/2}+e^{-c_{1}d})\leq C^{*}

for some
C^{*}
depending on
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
only. Thus, since
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X)\geq C_{4}
for some constant
C_{4}
large by our hypothesis
\mathfrak{z}\geq 1/\pi((0,k])
, we conclude that
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X^{2})\leq\frac{4}{3}(\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X))^{2}
, so

\mathbb{V}_{\pi}(X)\leq\frac{1}{3}(\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X))^{2}
.
Thus, by Chebyshev’s inequality,

\mathbb{P}(|X-\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X)|\geq\tfrac{3}{4}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X))\leq\mathbb{V}_{\pi}(X)/(\tfrac{3}{4}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(X))^{2}\leq\tfrac{16}{27},

and the statement follows taking
\widetilde{c}:=\frac{1}{4}
and
\widetilde{\eta}:=\frac{11}{27}
.
∎

To conclude this section, we show that the process we studied
in Sections
4.1
and
4.2

satisfies
(H1)
through
(H3)
and hence Proposition
27
holds for the said process. Reaching such conclusion was the motivation for this section, and it provides a result on which the analysis of the combined radial and angular processes, which we will treat in the next section, relies.

Corollary 28
.

Let
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
be the diffusion process on
(0,Y]
with
generator
\Delta_{h}
and a reflecting barrier at
Y
.
Then, there are constants
\widetilde{C}>0
,
\widetilde{c},\widetilde{\eta}\in(0,1)
, such that for all

\widetilde{C}\leq k\leq Y
and
\mathfrak{z}\geq 1/\pi((0,k])
we have

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}\big(I_{k}\geq\widetilde{c}\mathfrak{z}\pi((0,k])\big)\geq\widetilde{\eta},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where $I_{k}:=\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{z}}{\bf 1}_{(0,k]}(y_{s})ds$.}

Proof.

It will be enough to verify that
(H1)
-
(H3)
hold and apply
Proposition
27
with
\widetilde{C}
as therein and satisfying our just stated condition.

Let
C_{\alpha,Y}:=(\cosh(\alpha Y)-1)^{-1}
. By definition
\pi_{m}:=\pi((m{-}1,m])=C_{\alpha,Y}(\cosh(\alpha m)-\cosh(\alpha(m{-}1)))
.
Using
\cosh x-\cosh y=2\sinh(\frac{1}{2}(x+y))\sinh(\frac{1}{2}(x-y))
, we get

\pi_{m}=2C_{\alpha,Y}\sinh(\alpha(m-\tfrac{1}{2}))\sinh(\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq m\leq Y$}.

(20)

To show that
p
is bounded away from
0
as required by
(H1)
, it suffices
to observe that by Fact
26
,

p/q\geq p_{2}/q_{1}=\pi_{1}/\pi_{2}=\sinh(\frac{1}{2}\alpha)/\sinh(\frac{3}{2}\alpha)
. To establish that
p
is also bounded from above by a constant strictly smaller than
1/2
, recall the definition of
p^{\prime}_{m}
from this subsection’s introduction and let
q^{\prime}_{m}:=1-p^{\prime}_{m}
. Note that by definition of
q^{\prime}_{i}
, we have
q^{\prime}_{i}\pi_{i}=\pi_{i}-p^{\prime}_{i}\pi_{i}
, so from the proof of Fact
26
,
p^{\prime}_{m}\pi_{m}=q^{\prime}_{m-1}\pi_{m-1}=\pi_{m-1}-p^{\prime}_{m-2}\pi_{m-2}=\pi_{m-1}-\pi_{m-2}+p^{\prime}_{m-3}\pi_{m-3}
. Hence,
p^{\prime}_{m}\pi_{m}\leq\pi_{m-1}-\pi_{m-2}+\pi_{m-3}
for
3<m\leq Y
. It is easy to see that the right-hand side is, as a function in
\alpha
, maximized for
\alpha=1/2
. Moreover, for every
\alpha
, it is increasing in
m
, we see it is bounded from above by
0.4618
. By direct calculation,
p^{\prime}_{2}=\pi_{1}/\pi_{2}=\sinh(\alpha/2)/\sinh(3\alpha/2)\leq 0.3072
and
p^{\prime}_{3}=(1-p^{\prime}_{2})\pi_{2}/\pi_{3}\leq 0.3557
, where we used that both expressions for
p^{\prime}_{2}
and
p^{\prime}_{3}
are decreasing as functions in
\alpha
. We conclude that
\sup_{1\leq m\leq Y}p^{\prime}_{m}\leq 0.4618
for all
1<m\leq Y
. To conclude that
p<1/2
, we also need to bound
\widehat{p}_{m}
, for
1\leq m<Y
: in order to do so, observe that the diffusion process defined by (
2
) can be coupled with a process of constant drift towards
m+1
so that the real process is always to the right of the process of constant drift; for a process with constant drift towards
m+1
starting at
m
it is well known that the probability to reach
m-1
before
m+1
is at most
1/2-\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon>0
(see
[
11
]
, formula 3.0.4).
Hence
\widehat{p}_{m}\leq\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
for any
1\leq m<Y
, and hence
p<1/2
, as desired.

Next, we establish
(H2)
. Recalling that
T_{(Y-1)\pm 1}
is the random variable counting the maximal time the process spends in the interval
(Y-2,Y]
starting at
Y-1
; by Part (
ii
) of Lemma
14
applied with
{\bf y}_{0}:=Y-2
, for any
y\in(Y-2,Y]
, we get
\mathbb{E}_{y}(T_{{\bf y}_{0}})\leq\frac{4}{\alpha^{2}}e^{2\alpha}
, and since
\sup_{Y-2\leq y\leq Y}\mathbb{E}_{y}(T_{y\pm 1})\leq\sup_{Y-2<y\leq Y}\mathbb{E}_{y}(T_{{\bf y}_{0}})
, conditon
(H2)
is satisfied for
y>Y-2
. In order to bound
\mathbb{E}_{y}(T_{y\pm 1})
for
y<Y-1
, note that the time to exit from such an interval can only increase when imposing a reflecting barrier at
y+1
, and we can then apply Part (
ii
) of Lemma
14
to the process with this reflecting barrier at
y
, applied with
{\bf y}_{0}:=y-1
. Hence, for any starting position
y
, the expected time remaining in the interval
(y-1,y+1]
is at most a constant
L
, and
(H2)
is satisfied.

Finally, to check that
(H3)
holds, simply observe that
for all
\widetilde{C}\leq k\leq Y
(where
\widetilde{C}
is at least a constant strictly greater than
1
), the process
\{y_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
dominates a process of constant drift towards
Y
, which wherever conditioned on
y_{s}\leq k
has a constant probability of staying within
(0,k]
during the unit length time interval
[s,s+1]
, thus establishing the claim and concluding the proof of the corollary.
∎

5
General case: strategies for detection

In this section we define the set
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
similarly as in the radial section, without the restriction that
|\theta_{0}|\leq\tfrac{\pi}{2}
, since also points that belong to the half disk of
B_{O}(R)
opposite to
Q
are starting positions from which particles can detect (mainly thanks to the angular movement component). As in previous sections, on a high level, this set
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
is chosen in such a way that at least a constant part of the measure of the overall detection probability comes from
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
. The precise definition of
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
(given in the two following theorems) depends on the fact whether
\mathfrak{z}
is small or large. For
\mathfrak{z}
large, we can moreover provide uniform lower bounds of detection for every element in
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
and matching uniform upper bounds of detection for every element in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
. To make this intuition precise, we have the following two theorems, depending on whether
\mathfrak{z}
is small or large:

Theorem 29
.

Assume
\beta\leq 1/2
,
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega((e^{\beta R}/n)^{2})\cap O(1)
. Let

\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}:=\{x_{0}=(r_{0},\theta_{0})\in B_{O}(R)\mid|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(r_{0})+\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta r_{0}}\}.

(21)

Then

\int_{\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})=\Theta(\mu(\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}))=\Theta(ne^{-\beta R}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}})

and

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})=O(\mu(\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})})).

Theorem 30
.

Let
\kappa_{A}>1
. Define then
\kappa_{R}:=\kappa_{A}
if
\alpha<2\beta
and
\kappa_{R}:=e^{\kappa_{A}^{2}}
if
\alpha\geq 2\beta
. Let

\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}(\kappa_{A}):=\{x_{0}=(r_{0},\theta_{0})\in B_{O}(R)\mid|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\vee|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(r_{0})+\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r_{0}}\},

where

\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}:=\begin{cases}(\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}/e^{R})^{\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2}},&\text{if $\alpha<2\beta$},\\[2.0pt]
e^{-\beta R}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}\log\mathfrak{z}},&\text{if $\alpha=2\beta$},\\[2.0pt]
e^{-\beta R}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}},&\text{if $\alpha>2\beta$}.\end{cases}

Furthermore, define

\mathfrak{Z}:=\begin{cases}e^{\alpha R},&\text{if $\alpha<2\beta$,}\\
e^{\alpha R}/(\alpha R),&\text{if $\alpha=2\beta$,}\\
e^{2\beta R},&\text{if $\alpha>2\beta$.}\end{cases}

Then, for
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)\cap O(\mathfrak{Z})
we have

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}(\kappa_{A})}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})=O(\mu(\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}(\kappa_{A}))).

Furthermore, under the additional assumption
\mathfrak{z}=\omega(1)
we have

\inf_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}(\kappa_{A})}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\begin{cases}e^{-O(\kappa_{A}^{2})},&\text{ if $\alpha\geq 2\beta$,}\\[2.0pt]
\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha/(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})}),&\text{ if $\alpha<2\beta$,}\end{cases}

and

\sup_{x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}(\kappa_{A})}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\begin{cases}e^{-\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{2})},&\text{ if $\alpha\geq 2\beta$,}\\[2.0pt]
O(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha/(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})}),&\text{ if $\alpha<2\beta$.}\end{cases}

x_{\mathfrak{z}}
x_{0}
{}_{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}
{}_{\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\theta}_{0}}
{}_{\theta_{0}}
{}_{\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}}
{}_{\phi(r_{0})}
{}_{r_{0}}
{}_{r^{\prime\prime}}
{}_{r^{\prime}}
{}_{\widehat{r}_{0}}
Q
O

Figure 3:
(a) The lightly shaded area corresponds to
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}(\kappa_{A})\setminus B_{Q}(R)
as defined in Theorem
30
and the strongly shaded region represents
B_{Q}(R)
. (b) The smallest (respectively largest) circumference whose
boundary is a dashed line is of radius
r^{\prime}

(
r^{\prime\prime}
, respectively).
(c) The region whose boundary is the dashed-dot line corresponds to the box
\mathcal{B}(x_{0}):=[\widehat{r}_{0},R]\times[\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0},\theta_{0}+\widehat{\theta}_{0}]
when
\mathfrak{z}
is large.

We refer to Figure
3
(a) for an illustration of
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}(\kappa_{A})
as defined in the last theorem.

The following subsection is dedicated to proving the lower bounds of the two theorems just stated (in fact, as can be seen from the proof, the assumptions about
\mathfrak{z}
are slightly milder in the proofs). The subsequent subsection then deals with the corresponding upper bounds.

5.1
Lower bounds

In this subsection we prove the lower bounds of Theorem
29
and
30
.

5.1.1
The case
\mathfrak{z}
small

We start with the proof of the lower bound of Theorem
29
.
We establish the following proposition, which proves the lower bound of Theorem
29
.

Proposition 31
.

Fix
\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}
and take
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
as in (
21
). If
\mathfrak{z}=O(1)
, then

\int_{\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})\;=\;\Omega(ne^{-\beta R}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}).

Proof.

Consider
x_{0}
with
|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(r_{0})+\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta r_{0}}
and
r_{0}\geq c
for some constant
c>0
. Note that for such choice of
r_{0}
we have
\coth(r_{0})\leq\coth(c)=O(1)
and thus within time
O(1)
, with probability bounded away from zero, the radial coordinate changes by at most
1
. Conditioning on this, we consider the angular movement variance
1
Brownian motion
B_{I(\mathfrak{z})}
where now
I(\mathfrak{z}):=\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{z}}\cosech^{2}(\beta r_{\mathfrak{z}})d\mathfrak{z}\geq(\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}/\sinh(\beta(r_{0}+1))^{2}
. As in the proof of Theorem
9
, define the exit time
H_{[-a,b]}
from the interval
[-a,b]
where

a:=\phi(r_{0}+1)-|\theta_{0}|
and
b:=2\pi-\phi(r_{0}+1)-|\theta_{0}|
, and as in (
10
) in the proof of Theorem
9
,

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})\geq\mathbb{P}(H_{[-a,b]}\leq I(\mathfrak{z}))=\Omega\big(\Phi\big((\phi(r_{0}+1)-|\theta_{0}|)\sinh(\beta(r_{0}+1))/\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}\big)\big).

Since
\phi(\cdot)>0
and
\sinh(x)\leq e^{x}
we conclude that
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\Omega(\Phi(-|\theta_{0}|e^{\beta(r_{0}+1)}/\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}))
.
Integrating over the elements of
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
satisfying
|\theta_{0}|\leq\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta r_{0}}
,
we have

\int_{\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})=\Omega(ne^{-\alpha R})\int_{1}^{R}\int_{0}^{\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta\widehat{r}_{0}}}\Phi(-\theta_{0}e^{\beta\widehat{r}_{0}}/\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}})\sinh(\alpha\widehat{r}_{0})d\theta_{0}d\widehat{r}_{0}.

Performing the change of variables
y_{0}:=\theta_{0}e^{\beta\widehat{r}_{0}}/\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}
, since
\alpha>\beta
, and using
\sinh(x)=\Theta(e^{x})
for
x=\Omega(1)
, we get for the latter

\Omega(ne^{-\alpha R})\int_{0}^{1}\Phi(-y_{0})\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}dy_{0}\int_{1}^{R}e^{(\alpha-\beta)\widehat{r}_{0}}d\widehat{r}_{0}=\Omega(ne^{-\beta R}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}})\int_{0}^{1}\Phi(-y_{0})dy_{0}.

Note that the last integral is
\Omega(1)
, and thus the stated result follows.
∎

5.1.2
The case
\mathfrak{z}
large

We now prove the lower bound of Theorem
30
. In fact, we will only need to assume the milder condition
\mathfrak{z}\geq Ce^{2\kappa_{A}^{2}}
with
C
being a large enough constant. We start by showing that particles that start close to the origin detect quickly with at least constant probability:

Lemma 32
.

Let
\tau>0
, and let
c^{*}:=C^{*}/\beta
for some arbitrarily large constant
C^{*}>0
. Then,

\inf_{x_{0}\in B_{O}(c^{*})}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\tau)=\Omega(1).

Proof.

Let
T_{h}
be the smallest time
t
such that
r_{t}=2c^{*}
and notice that, calling
\mathbb{P}^{r}
the law of the radial component of the process, for any point
x_{0}=(r_{0},\theta_{0})
with
r_{0}\leq c^{*}
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}^{r}_{r_{0}}(T_{h}\geq\tau)\,\geq\,\mathbb{P}^{r}_{c^{*}}(T_{h}\geq\tau).

Now, fix any realization
\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
of the radial component of the process such that
T_{h}\geq\tau
and observe that for any such realization, detection is guaranteed if
|\theta_{s}-\theta_{0}|>2\pi
for some
0\leq s\leq\tau
. Under the event
T_{h}\geq\tau
the radial coordinate at any time
s\leq\tau
is at most
2c^{*}
. Thus, the angular movement
\theta_{\tau}-\theta_{0}
has a normal distribution centered at zero with variance at least

\int_{0}^{\tau}\sinh^{-2}(\beta r_{s})ds\,\geq\,\tau\sinh^{-2}(2C^{*})=\Omega(1).

Thus, with constant probability, within time
\tau
the angular movement covers an angle of
2\pi
. The lemma follows.
∎

We now deal with the remaining starting points
x_{0}\in\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
.
Before doing so we establish a simple fact concerning the random variables defined for nonnegative integer values of
k
as follows:

I_{k}:=\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{z}}{\bf 1}_{(0,k]}(r_{s})ds.

(22)

Recall that
\pi(r):=\frac{\alpha\sinh(\alpha r)}{\cosh(\alpha R)-1}
,
0\leq r\leq R
, is the stationary distribution of the process
\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
.

Fact 33
.

If
k\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\log 2
,
then
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(I_{k})\geq\tfrac{1}{4}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha(R-k)}
.

Proof.

Suppose
r_{0}
is distributed according to the stationary distribution
\pi
.
Since
\cosh(x)-1\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{x}
, by definition of
I_{k}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(I_{k})=\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{z}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(r_{s}\leq k)ds=\mathfrak{z}\pi((0,k])=\mathfrak{z}\cdot\frac{\cosh(\alpha k)-1}{\cosh(\alpha R)-1}\geq 2\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}(\cosh(\alpha k)-1).

The desired conclusion follows observing that the map
x\mapsto 1+e^{-2x}-2e^{-x}
is non-decreasing, and thus for
x\geq\log 2
, we have

\cosh(x)-1=\frac{1}{2}e^{x}(1+e^{-2x}-2e^{-x})\geq\frac{1}{4}e^{x}
.
∎

We are now ready to state and prove the proposition dealing with particles whose initial position are points satisfying the first condition in the definition of
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
:

Proposition 34
.

Let
\kappa_{A}>1
. There is a sufficiently large constant
C>0
such that if
\mathfrak{z}\geq Ce^{2\kappa_{A}^{2}}
and
\mathfrak{z}=O(\mathfrak{Z})
, then for any
x_{0}=(r_{0},\theta_{0})
with
|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, we have the following:

(i)

If
\alpha\geq 2\beta
, then
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\Omega(\tfrac{1}{\kappa_{A}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{A}^{2}})=\Omega(e^{-0.7\kappa_{A}^{2}})
.

(ii)

If
\alpha<2\beta
, then
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha/(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})})
.

To prove the just stated proposition we use the following standard fact several times, so we state it explicitly.

Fact 35

(
[
20
]
)
.

Given the radial component
\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
of a particle’s trajectory, the angular component
\{\theta_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
law is that of a Brownian motion indexed by
I(\mathfrak{z}):=\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{z}}\cosech^{2}(\beta r_{s})ds
.
If
I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma^{2}>0
and
\kappa>0
, then

\mathbb{P}(\sup_{0\leq s\leq\mathfrak{z}}B_{I(s)}\geq\kappa\sigma\mid\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0})\geq\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2\pi}(\kappa^{2}+1)}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\kappa^{2}}=\Omega\Big(\frac{1}{\kappa}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\kappa^{2}}\Big).

Now we proceed with the pending proof.

Proof of Proposition
34
.

Assume first
\beta\geq 1/2

(and thus necessarily
\alpha<2\beta
). In this case the radial movement dominates and the proof of (
ii
) is very similar to the one given for
x_{0}\in\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
where
|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(R)+\kappa\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
: assume first that
\mathfrak{z}
(and
\kappa_{A}
) is such that
|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\leq\pi/2-c
for some
c>0
. Define
\overline{r}_{0}
be as
\mathfrak{r}_{0}
in the proof of Proposition
20
(that is
\overline{r}_{0}
corresponds to the absorption radius in case there was radial movement only; since
\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\leq\pi/2-c
, we have
\overline{r}_{0}=\Omega(1)
). By the same argument as given in the proof of Proposition
20
, with
\kappa_{A}
playing the role of
\kappa
therein, with probability
\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{-2\alpha})
, there exists a time moment
T\leq\mathfrak{z}
, at which a radial value of
\overline{r}_{0}
is reached. In this case, by symmetry of the angular movement, with probability at least
1/2
, either the angle at time
T
satisfies
|\theta_{T}|\leq|\theta_{0}|
or there exists
t\leq T
where the angle
\theta_{t}=0
and we detected already by time
t
, and hence in this case
Q
is detected by time
T
with probability
\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{-2\alpha})
. Assume then that
\mathfrak{z}
(and
\kappa_{A}
) is such that
\pi/2-c\leq\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
. In this case, let
\overline{r}_{0}
be equal (assuming there were radial movement only) to the absorption radius
\mathfrak{r}_{0}
that would correspond to an angle exactly
|\theta_{0}|=\pi/2-c
. Note that in this case
\overline{r}_{0}=\Theta(1)
. By the proof of Proposition
20
, with probability
\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{-2\alpha})
a radial value of
\overline{r}_{0}
is reached at a time moment
T\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}
. In this case, with probability at least
1/2
, as before, either there existed a moment
t
with
\theta_{t}=0
, and we would have detected
Q
, or
|\theta_{T}|\leq|\theta_{0}|
. In this case, by Lemma
32
, since
\overline{r}_{0}=\Theta(1)
, with constant probability we detect
Q
in time
\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
, and hence also in this case
Q
is detected by time
\mathfrak{z}
with probability
\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{-2\alpha})
.

Consider thus
x_{0}\in\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
with
|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
under the assumption
\beta<1/2
. Recall that we also may assume that
r_{0}\geq c^{*}
for
c^{*}
as in the statement of Lemma
32
since other values of
r_{0}
were already dealt with in said lemma.
First, note that for
x_{0}\in B_{Q}(R)
the lower bound trivially holds.
So, henceforth let
x_{0}\in\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}\setminus B_{Q}(R)
. Since by hypothesis
\mathfrak{z}\in O(\mathfrak{Z})=O(e^{\alpha R})
, we may assume that
\mathfrak{z}<\exp(\alpha(R-\frac{1}{\alpha}\log 2-1+c))
for
c>0
sufficiently large. Let
\rho:=R-\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\mathfrak{z}+c
and note that
\frac{1}{\alpha}\log 2+1<\rho
by the previous assumption on
\mathfrak{z}
.
Denote by
T_{\rho-1}
the random variable corresponding to the first time the process reaches
B_{O}(\rho-1)
. Recall from Part (
ii
) of Lemma
14
, with
{\bf y}_{0}=\rho-1
and
Y=R
, that for the radial component
\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
of the movement, and for any
r_{0}\in[\rho-1,R]
,

\mathbb{E}_{r_{0}}(T_{\rho-1})\leq\frac{4}{\alpha^{2}}e^{\alpha(R-\rho+1)}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{2}}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha(c-1)}.

By Markov’s inequality, for
c
sufficiently large so that
\frac{4}{\alpha^{2}}e^{-\alpha(c-1)}\leq\frac{1}{4}
, it follows that the event
\mathcal{A}
corresponding to the process reaching
B_{O}(\rho-1)

before time
\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}
happens with
probability

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A})=1-\mathbb{P}_{r_{0}}(T_{\rho-1}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z})\geq\tfrac{1}{2}.

Now, let
\overline{\rho}:=\max\{\rho-\beta\log\kappa_{A},c^{*}\}.
Moreover, define
\mathcal{B}
as the event that starting from radius
\rho-1
we hit the radial value
\overline{\rho}
before hitting the radial value
R
.
Define
g(r):=\log(\tanh(\frac{1}{2}\alpha R)/\tanh(\frac{1}{2}\alpha r))
as in Fact
17
and observe that as argued therein,
since
\rho=\Omega(1)
we have
g(\rho-1)=O(e^{-\alpha\rho})
and because

\overline{\rho}=R-\Omega(1)
we have
g(\overline{\rho})=\Omega(e^{-\alpha\overline{\rho}})
. Recall from Part (
iii
) of Lemma
14
that
g(\rho-1)/g(\overline{\rho})
is the probability that starting from radius
\rho-1
we hit the radial value
\overline{\rho}
before hitting the radial value
R
, and we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B})=\frac{g(\rho-1)}{g(\overline{\rho})}=\Omega(e^{\alpha(\overline{\rho}-\rho)})=\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha/\beta}).

From Part (
iv
) of Lemma
14
we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{\rho-1}(T_{\overline{\rho}}\mid T_{\overline{\rho}}<T_{R})\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}(\beta\log\kappa_{A}+1)+\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\leq\frac{\mathfrak{z}}{8},

where the last inequality follows (with room to spare) by our assumption of
\mathfrak{z}
.
Thus, by Markov’s inequality, conditionally under
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
,
with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
, we reach
\overline{\rho}
before time
\frac{3}{4}\mathfrak{z}
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the corresponding event. Conditional under
\mathcal{C}
, with constant probability the particle stays one unit of time inside
B_{O}(\overline{\rho}+1)
before time
\mathfrak{z}
, and let this event be called
\mathcal{D}
. Conditional under
\mathcal{D}
, the angular component’s law during one unit of time inside
B_{O}(\overline{\rho}+1)
is
B_{I(\mathfrak{z})}
with

I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\cosech^{2}(\beta(\overline{\rho}+1))\geq e^{-2\beta(\overline{\rho}+1)}\geq e^{-2\beta(R-\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\mathfrak{z}+c-\frac{1}{\beta}\log\kappa_{A}+1)}=:\sigma^{2}.

Note that
\sigma=e^{-\beta R}\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}\kappa_{A}e^{-\beta(c+1)}
for the absolute constant
c>0
independent of
\kappa_{A}
from above, and recall also that

|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}e^{-\beta R}\leq 2\kappa_{A}\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}e^{-\beta R}
by assumptions on
\beta<1/2
and
\mathfrak{z}
, with room to spare.
Let
\mathcal{E}
be the event that when reaching
B_{O}(\overline{\rho}+1)
the angle at the origin spanned by the particle and
Q
is (in absolute value) is at most
|\theta_{0}|
or there was a moment
t
before reaching
B_{O}(\overline{\rho}+1)
with
\theta_{t}=0
(and thus we detected at time
t
). Note that by symmetry of the angular movement,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
Conditional under
\mathcal{D}\cap\mathcal{E}
, by Fact
35
, since
\kappa_{A}>1
, for some
c_{1}=c_{1}(c)
we have

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})\geq\mathbb{P}(\sup_{0\leq s\leq\mathfrak{z}}B_{I(s)}\geq c_{1}\sigma\mid\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0})=\Omega(1),

and since
\mathcal{D}\cap\mathcal{E}
holds with probability
\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha/\beta})
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha/\beta})
,
which finishes the proof of the case
\alpha<2\beta
and
\beta<1/2
(and thus the proof of (
ii
)).

We now deal with the remaining
\alpha\geq 2\beta
case (and therefore necessarily
\beta<1/2
). Assume first
\alpha>2\beta
. This argument is analogous to the one for angular movement: we repeat it for convenience.
Given the trajectory of
\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
, recall that the angular component’s law is that of a Brownian motion
B_{I(\mathfrak{z})}
, where
I(\mathfrak{z}):=\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{z}}\cosech^{2}(\beta r_{s})ds\geq 4\mathfrak{z}e^{-2\beta R}=:\sigma^{2}
.
Hence, using Fact
35
, since
\kappa_{A}>1
and using that
0.2x^{2}\geq\log x
for all
x\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})\geq\mathbb{P}(\sup_{0\leq s\leq\mathfrak{z}}B_{I(s)}\geq\kappa_{A}\sigma\mid\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0})=\Omega\big(-\tfrac{1}{\kappa_{A}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{A}^{2}}\big)={\Omega(e^{-0.7\kappa_{A}^{2}})},

showing the result in the case
\alpha>2\beta
.

Finally, suppose
\alpha=2\beta
(therefore
\beta>\frac{1}{4}
).
First, suppose that the starting point
r_{0}
of the radial component is distributed according to the stationary distribution of the process, that is,
\pi(r):=\frac{\alpha\sinh(\alpha r)}{\cosh(\alpha R)-1}
with
0\leq r\leq R
.
We will see that the contribution to
I(\mathfrak{z}):=\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{z}}\cosech^{2}(\beta r_{s})ds
of the time spent around different radial values is roughly the same, forcing a logarithmic correction. To bound
I(\mathfrak{z})
from below, let
\overline{k}=R-\lfloor\tfrac{1}{\alpha}\log(\mathfrak{z}/4)\rfloor
, which by our hypothesis
\mathfrak{z}=O(\mathfrak{Z})=O(e^{\alpha R}/(\alpha R))
implies
\overline{k}=\omega(1)
(in particular it implies also
\overline{k}\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\log 2
).
Note that

I(\mathfrak{z})=\int_{0}^{\overline{k}}\cosech^{2}(\beta r_{s})ds+\!\sum_{k=\overline{k}+1}^{R}\int_{k-1}^{k}\cosech^{2}(\beta r_{s})ds\geq 4\Big(I_{\overline{k}}e^{-2\beta\overline{k}}+\!\sum_{k=\overline{k}+1}^{R}(I_{k}-I_{k-1})e^{-2\beta k}\Big).

Hence, using that
\beta\geq\frac{1}{4}
implies that
4(1-e^{-2\beta})\geq 1
,

I(\mathfrak{z})\geq 4\Big(\sum_{k=\overline{k}}^{R-1}I_{k}e^{-2\beta k}(1-e^{-2\beta})+I_{R}e^{-2\beta R}\Big)\geq\sum_{k=\overline{k}}^{R}e^{-2\beta k}I_{k}.

(23)

So, recalling that
\overline{k}\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\log 2
, by Fact
33
, for all
k\in\{\overline{k},\ldots,R\}
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(I_{k})\geq\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha(R-k)}
,
which gives an estimate for the value of the
I_{k}
variables. Moreover, by Corollary
28
, since
k\geq\overline{k}
and by definition of
\overline{k}
we have
\mathfrak{z}\geq 4e^{\alpha(R-\overline{k})}\geq 4e^{\alpha(R-k)}
. Since
\cosh(x)-1=\frac{1}{2}e^{x}(1-e^{-x})^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{x}
, using once more that
k\geq\overline{k}\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\log 2
, we obtain
\pi((0,k])\geq e^{-\alpha(R-k)}(1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha k})^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}e^{-\alpha(R-k)}
, so
\mathfrak{z}\geq 1/\pi((0,k])
and the assumptions of Corollary
28
are satisfied. Hence, there exist
\widetilde{c},\widetilde{\eta}\in(0,1)
, such that for each
\overline{k}\leq k\leq R
, we have that the expectation of the indicator
Z_{k}
of the event
\{I_{k}\geq\widetilde{c}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha(R-k)}\}
is at least
\widetilde{\eta}
. Let
Z:=\sum_{k=\overline{k}}^{R}Z_{k}
and that
\mathbb{E}(Z)\geq(R-\overline{k}+1)\widetilde{\eta}
. Define then the event

\mathcal{E}:=\{Z>\frac{\widetilde{\eta}}{2}(R-\overline{k}+1)\}
. Since
\mathbb{E}(Z)\leq(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})+\frac{\widetilde{\eta}}{2}\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}))(R-\overline{k}+1)
,
it must be the case that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\geq\frac{\widetilde{\eta}}{2}/(1-\frac{\widetilde{\eta}}{2})\geq\frac{\widetilde{\eta}}{2}
.
Thus, for a fixed realization of
\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
satisfying
\mathcal{E}
, since
\alpha=2\beta
, we have

\sum_{k=\overline{k}}^{R}e^{-2\beta k}I_{k}\geq\sum_{k=\overline{k}}^{R}e^{-2\beta k}\widetilde{c}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha(R-k)}Z_{k}=\widetilde{c}\mathfrak{z}e^{-2\beta R}\sum_{k=\overline{k}}^{R}Z_{k}>\widetilde{c}\mathfrak{z}e^{-2\beta R}\frac{\widetilde{\eta}}{2}(R-\overline{k}+1).

Note that
R-\overline{k}+1\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\log(\mathfrak{z}/4)
by definition of
\overline{k}
, so

\sum_{k=\overline{k}}^{R}e^{-2\beta k}I_{k}\geq\frac{\widetilde{c}}{2\alpha}\widetilde{\eta}\mathfrak{z}e^{-2\beta R}\log(\mathfrak{z}/4))\geq\frac{\widetilde{c}}{3\alpha}\widetilde{\eta}\mathfrak{z}e^{-2\beta R}\log\mathfrak{z},

where we used the assumption that
\mathfrak{z}
is at least a sufficiently large constant.
Thus, under
\mathcal{E}
the angular movement dominates stochastically
a Brownian motion
B_{\frac{\widetilde{c}}{3\alpha}\widetilde{\eta}\mathfrak{z}e^{-2\beta R}\log\mathfrak{z}}
with standard deviation

e^{-\beta R}\sqrt{(\widetilde{c}\mathfrak{z}\widetilde{\eta}/(3\alpha))\log\mathfrak{z}}=:\sigma
. By Fact
35
,

\mathbb{P}(\sup_{0\leq s\leq\mathfrak{z}}B_{I(s)}\geq\kappa_{A}\sigma\mid\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0})=\Omega(\tfrac{1}{\kappa_{A}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{A}^{2}}).

Thus, for
x_{0}\in\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
with
|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\leq 2\kappa_{A}e^{-\beta R}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}\log\mathfrak{z}}
, since
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\mathcal{E})\geq\frac{\widetilde{\eta}}{2}
and using that
0.2x^{2}\geq\log x
for all
x\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})\geq\tfrac{\widetilde{\eta}}{2}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq \mathfrak{z}\mid\mathcal{E})=\Omega(\tfrac{1}{\kappa_{A}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{A}^{2}})=\Omega(e^{-0.7\kappa_{A}^{2}}).

(24)

It remains to show thus that with positive probability the trajectory starting with a fixed initial radius for
x_{0}\in\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
can be coupled in such a way that the probability of detection of the target by time
\mathfrak{z}
can be bounded from below by the probability of detection when the radius is chosen according to the stationary distribution
\pi(\cdot)
. Denote by
\widehat{r}_{t}
the radial component at time
t
when starting according to the stationary distribution.
Consider the event
\mathcal{A}
that the initial radial value
\widehat{r}_{0}
is at most one unit away from the
boundary of
B_{O}(R)
, that is,
\mathcal{A}:=\{\widehat{r}_{0}\in[R-1,R]\}
.
Clearly,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A})=\pi([R-1,R])=\Omega(1)
.
Let
\mathcal{B}
be the event that
\{\widehat{r}_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
starting from
R-1
hits
R
by time
\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}
.
Conditional under
\mathcal{A}
, since the time to hit
R
is clearly dominated by the time a standard Brownian motion (corresponding to a one-dimensional radial movement) hits
R
starting from
R-1
, by our lower bound on
\mathfrak{z}
, we have that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}\mid\mathcal{A})=\Omega(1)
. Note that conditional under
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
either the trajectories starting with a fixed value of
r_{0}
on the one hand and with
\widehat{r}_{0}
according to the stationary distribution
\pi(\cdot)
on the other hand must cross by time
\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}
(and they can be coupled naturally from then on for a time interval of
\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}
), or
r_{t}\leq\widehat{r}_{t}
for any
0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}
, and during an initial time period of length
\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}
the detection probability starting from
r_{0}
stochastically dominates the one when starting from
\widehat{r}_{0}
. Thus, with probability
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B})=\Omega(1)
, the process
\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
can be successfully coupled with
\{\widehat{r}_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
, and since we aim for a lower bound, we assume that
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
holds. Conditional under
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
, we may thus apply the reasoning yielding (
24
) with
\mathfrak{z}
replaced by
\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}
, and the result follows.
∎

We still have to deal with particles whose initial location are points satisfying the second condition in the definition of
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
. The next proposition does this.

Proposition 36
.

Let
\kappa_{R}>1
and assume that
\mathfrak{z}\geq Ce^{2\kappa_{A}^{2}}
for
C
large enough.
Then, for any
x_{0}=(r_{0},\theta_{0})
with

(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))e^{(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r_{0}}\leq\kappa_{R}
, we have the following:

(i)

If
\alpha\geq 2\beta
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\Omega(\kappa_{R}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}})=\Omega(e^{-{\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\kappa_{A}^{2}}}).

(ii)

If
\alpha<2\beta
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\Omega(\kappa_{R}^{-\alpha/(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})})=\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha/(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})}).

Proof.

Thanks to Lemma
32
we may, and will, assume throughout the proof that

r_{0}>c^{*}
for an arbitrarily large
c^{*}
. Under said condition, the proof argument formalizes the following intuitive fact: Either there is a chance for the particle to move radially towards the origin so that the boundary of
B_{Q}(R)
is reached and detection happens, or there is a chance to move radially towards the origin, enough so that the particle stays long enough in such a region close enough to the origin so that the relatively large angle the particle traverses makes detection probable.

We first assume
\beta<1/2
.
Observe that we may assume
|\theta_{0}|\geq 2\phi(r_{0})
, since otherwise during one time unit there is constant probability that the radial value is at most
\min\{R,r_{0}+1\}
, and conditional under this event, during this time unit with constant probability an angular movement of standard deviation at least
e^{-\beta(r_{0}+1)}
is performed, thus covering with constant probability an angular distance of
e^{-\beta(r_{0}+1)}\geq 2\phi(r_{0})
in the case of
\beta<1/2
for
r_{0}>c^{*}
. We may and will thus below replace the condition
(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))e^{(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r_{0}}\leq\kappa_{R}
by
|\theta_{0}|e^{(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r_{0}}\leq 2\kappa_{R}
. We consider the worst-case scenario, i.e.,
|\theta_{0}|=2\kappa_{R}e^{-\beta r_{0}}
, or equivalently
r_{0}=\frac{1}{\beta}\log(2\kappa_{R}/|\theta_{0}|)
. We restrict our discussion to vertices with
r_{0}<R-\log\kappa_{A}
, as other vertices were already considered before: indeed, for values of
r_{0}\geq R-\log\kappa_{A}
, in case (
i
)
we have
2\kappa_{R}e^{-\beta r_{0}}=2e^{\kappa_{A}^{2}}e^{-\beta r_{0}}\leq e^{\kappa_{A}^{2}}e^{-\beta R}\kappa_{A}\leq\kappa_{A}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta R}=\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, where the second inequality follows from our assumption of
\mathfrak{z}\geq Ce^{2\kappa_{A}^{2}}
with
C
large. Hence such values of
x_{0}=(\theta_{0},r_{0})
satisfy the first condition of
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
and were treated in Proposition
34
. In case (
ii
), for values of
r_{0}\geq R-\log\kappa_{A}
, we have
2\kappa_{R}e^{-\beta r_{0}}=2\kappa_{A}e^{-\beta r_{0}}\leq 2\kappa_{A}^{2}e^{-\beta R}\leq\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, where again the second inequality follows from the same assumption on
\mathfrak{z}\geq Ce^{2\kappa_{A}^{2}}
(again with room to spare), and again this case was already dealt with in Proposition
34
.
Define
g(r):=\log(\tanh(\frac{1}{2}\alpha R)/\tanh(\frac{1}{2}\alpha r))
as in Fact
17
and let
\overline{r}_{0}:=\max\{r_{0}-\frac{1}{\beta}\log\kappa_{R},c^{*}\}
. By arguments given in said fact, since we have restricted our discussion to the case where
r_{0}=R-\Omega(1)
, we have
g(r_{0})=\Omega(e^{-\alpha r_{0}})
and, since
\overline{r}_{0}\geq c^{*}
with
c^{*}
large, we also have
g(\overline{r}_{0})=O(e^{-\alpha\overline{r}_{0}})
. Recall from Part (
iii
) of Lemma
14
that
g(r_{0})/g(\overline{r}_{0})
is the probability that starting from radius
r_{0}
we hit the radial value
\overline{r}_{0}
before hitting the radial value
R
, and we have

\frac{g(r_{0})}{g(\overline{r}_{0})}=\Omega(e^{\alpha(\overline{r}_{0}-r_{0})})=\Omega(e^{\alpha(\max\{r_{0}-\frac{1}{\beta}\log\kappa_{R},c^{*}\}-r_{0})})=\Omega(\kappa_{R}^{-\alpha/\beta}).

Thus, by Part (
iv
) of Lemma
14
with
y_{0}:=\overline{r}_{0}
and
Y:=R
, we have that
\mathbb{E}_{r_{0}}(T_{\overline{r}_{0}}\mid T_{\overline{r}_{0}}<T_{R})\leq\frac{2}{\alpha\beta}\log\kappa_{R}+\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{z}
by our lower bound hypothesis of
\mathfrak{z}
with
C:=C(\beta)
sufficiently large.
By Markov’s inequality, conditional under
T_{\overline{r}_{0}}<T_{R}
, starting at radius
r_{0}
, with probability at least
1/2
, the value of
\overline{r}_{0}
is hit by time
\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}
. In this case, if
\overline{r}_{0}=c^{*}
, then by Lemma
32
the target is detected with constant probability in constant time, and we are done. If
\overline{r}_{0}=r_{0}-\frac{1}{\beta}\log\kappa_{R}>c^{*}
, then with constant probability, during the ensuing unit time interval, the radial coordinate
r
is always at most
\overline{r}_{0}+1
. Call this event
\mathcal{A}
. By symmetry of the angular movement, with probability at least
1/2
, either the angle
\theta
at the hitting time of
\overline{r}_{0}
is in absolute value at most
|\theta_{0}|
or there was a time moment
t
with
\theta_{t}=0
before, and we had detected
Q
by time
t
already. Call this event
\mathcal{B}
. Conditional under
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
, the variance of the angular movement after reaching
\overline{r}_{0}
during the following unit time interval is at least

e^{-2\beta(r_{0}-\frac{1}{\beta}\log\kappa_{R}+1)}=e^{-2\beta(r_{0}+1)}\kappa_{R}^{2}
,
so recalling that by our worst-case assumption
|\theta_{0}|=2\kappa_{R}e^{-\beta r_{0}}
, the standard deviation is thus at least
e^{-\beta(r_{0}+1)}\kappa_{R}=e^{-\beta}|\theta_{0}|/2
. Hence, with constant probability, independently of
\kappa_{R}
(and also independently of
\beta
), in this case an angle of
|\theta_{0}|
is covered during a unit time interval, and by our lower bound assumption of
\mathfrak{z}
, the particle is detected by time
\mathfrak{z}
, finishing also this case and establishing the proposition when
\beta<1/2

by plugging in the respective relations between
\kappa_{A}
and
\kappa_{R}
in order to obtain the last equality of the the two parts of the statement.

Next, we consider Part (
ii
) which encompasses a case similar to the one analyzed in Section
4
, so we give only a short sketch of how it is handled. By hypothesis and case assumption
|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(r_{0})+\kappa_{R}e^{-\frac{1}{2}r_{0}}
. Since
\kappa_{R}>1
, we thus have
|\theta_{0}|\leq 3\kappa_{R}e^{-\frac{1}{2}r_{0}}
. Again, it suffices to show the statement for the worst-case scenario, that is, we may assume
|\theta_{0}|=3\kappa_{R}e^{-\frac{1}{2}r_{0}}
. We may assume that
r_{0}<R-\kappa_{R}
, since for vertices with
r_{0}\geq R-\kappa_{R}
, using our assumption of
\mathfrak{z}\geq Ce^{2\kappa_{A}^{2}}
(with room to spare) we have

3\kappa_{R}e^{-\frac{1}{2}r_{0}}\leq 3\kappa_{R}e^{\kappa_{R}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}R}\leq\kappa_{A}\mathfrak{z}^{1/(2\alpha)e^{-\frac{1}{2}R}}=\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, and such cases were already dealt with in the proof of Part (
ii
) of Proposition
34
. By Lemma
32
we may also assume
r_{0}>c^{*}
for arbitrarily large
c^{*}
.
Define
\overline{r}_{0}:=\max\{r_{0}-2\log\kappa_{R}-2,c^{*}\}
.
As before, recall from Part (
iii
) of Lemma
14
that
g(r_{0})/g(\overline{r}_{0})
is the probability that starting from radius
r_{0}
we hit the radial value
\overline{r}_{0}
before hitting the radial value
R
. Since
r_{0}=R-\Omega(1)
and since
