\overline{r}_{0}\geq c^{*}
, we have

\frac{g(r_{0})}{g(\overline{r}_{0})}=\Omega(e^{\alpha(\overline{r}_{0}-r_{0})})=\Omega(e^{\alpha(\max\{r_{0}-2\log\kappa_{R}-2,c^{*}\}-r_{0})})=\Omega(\kappa_{R}^{-2\alpha}),

and let
\mathcal{A}
be the event that this indeed happens.
By Part (
iv
) of Lemma
14
with
y_{0}:=\overline{r}_{0}
and
Y:=R
, we have that
\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}(T_{\overline{r}_{0}}\mid\mathcal{A})\leq\frac{4}{\alpha}\log\kappa_{R}+\frac{4}{\alpha}+\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{z}
by our lower bound hypothesis on
\mathfrak{z}
. By Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
1/2
,
T_{\overline{r}_{0}}<\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}
. Let
\mathcal{B}
be the event to reach the radius
\overline{r}_{0}
by time
\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}
.
We have

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\mathcal{B})\geq\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\mathcal{B}\mid\mathcal{A})\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\mathcal{A})=\Omega(\kappa_{R}^{-2\alpha}).

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the event that one of the following events occurs: Either at the moment
T
when reaching
\overline{r}_{0}
the angle made at the origin between the particle and
Q
is in absolute value at most
|\theta_{0}|
, or there was a moment
t\leq T
where
\theta_{t}=0
(and we already detected
Q
by time
t
). Note that by symmetry of the angular movement,
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\mathcal{C}\mid\mathcal{B})=\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\mathcal{C})\geq 1/2
. Hence,
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{B})=\Omega(\kappa_{R}^{-2\alpha})
. To conclude observe first the following:
If it is the case that
\overline{r}_{0}=c^{*}
, then conditional under
\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{B}
, by Lemma
32
, with constant probability, in time
O(1)
the particle
Q
is detected, and since
\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{z}+O(1)\leq\mathfrak{z}
, we are done. In particular, if
we had
|\theta_{0}|>\pi/2-c
for some sufficiently small
c>0
, then, since
|\theta_{0}|\leq 3\kappa_{R}e^{-\beta r_{0}}
, we must have
r_{0}<c^{*}
, and therefore clearly also
\overline{r}_{0}=c^{*}
. If on the other hand we had
|\theta_{0}|\leq\pi/2-c
for some arbitrarily small
c>0
and also
\overline{r}_{0}=r_{0}-2\log\kappa_{R}
, then observe that since
\overline{r}_{0}>c^{*}
with
c^{*}
large enough, we have

\phi(\overline{r}_{0})\geq e1.99\kappa_{R}e^{-r_{0}/2}\geq 3\kappa_{R}e^{-r_{0}/2}\geq|\theta_{0}|
, and
Q
is detected by time
T
, since
\phi(\overline{r}_{0})
corresponds to the maximum angle at the origin between a particle at radius
\overline{r}_{0}
and
Q
that guarantees detection.
∎

The uniform lower bound of Theorem
30
now follows directly by combining Proposition
34
and Proposition
36
. The integral lower bound of Theorem
30
follows directly from Proposition
34
applied with
\kappa_{A}
being any fixed constant and the fact that
\mu(\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})})=\Omega(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
(by considering the condition
|\theta_{0}|\leq\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
only), and the proofs of the lower bounds of Theorem
29
and Theorem
30
are finished.

5.2
Upper bounds

In this section we will show the corresponding upper bounds of Theorems
29
and
30
: that is, we show that particles initially placed outside
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, with
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
as in the corresponding theorems, have only a small chance of detecting the target by time
\mathfrak{z}
. For all values of
\mathfrak{z}
, we will show that

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})=O(\mu(\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})})),

thus establishing that the significant contribution to the tail probabilities comes from particles inside
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, and for large values of
\mathfrak{z}
we will show uniform upper bounds for the detection probability of every point outside
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
. In order to analyze the trajectory of a particle we will make use of the fact that the generator driving its motion can be separated into its radial and angular component, given respectively by

\Delta_{rad}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial r^{2}}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\frac{1}{\tanh(\alpha r)}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\Delta_{ang}=\frac{1}{2\sinh^{2}(\beta r)}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}.

Since the generator driving the radial part of the motion does not depend on
\theta
, our approach consists in sampling the radial component
\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
of the trajectories first and then, conditional under a given radial trajectory, sampling the angular component
\{\theta_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
. With this approach we can make use of the results obtained in Section
4
in order to study
\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
, while the angular component is distributed according to a time-changed Brownian motion
\theta_{s}=B_{I(s)}
, where

I(s):=\int_{0}^{s}\cosech^{2}(\beta r_{u})du

relates the radial trajectory to the angular variance of
\{\theta_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
, as already seen in Section
3
. To be able to apply the insight obtained by studying each component separately, we will need to replace the hitting time of the target (which translates into hitting
B_{Q}(R)
whose boundary defines a curve relating
r
and
\theta
) by the exit time of a simpler set: let
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
be any fixed initial location with
\theta_{0}\in(0,\pi)
and define a box
\mathcal{B}(x_{0})
containing
x_{0}
, of the form

\mathcal{B}(x_{0}):=[\widehat{r}_{0},R]\times[\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0},\theta_{0}+\widehat{\theta}_{0}]\subseteq\overline{B}_{Q}(R)

(25)

where
\widehat{\theta}_{0}
and
\widehat{r}_{0}
will be defined later on. Since
\mathcal{B}(x_{0})\subseteq\overline{B}_{Q}(R)
, it follows that in order for a particle starting at
x_{0}
to detect the target, it must first exit the box through either its upper boundary or its side boundaries. Denoting by
T^{rad}_{0}
and
T^{ang}_{0}
the respective hitting times of said boundaries, this implies that
T^{rad}_{0}\wedge T^{ang}_{0}\leq T_{det}
, and we can obtain the bound

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})\,\leq\,\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{rad}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})+\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{ang}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z}<T^{rad}_{0}).

(26)

The advantage of addressing the exit time of
\mathcal{B}(x_{0})
instead of
T_{det}
is straightforward; the first event
\{T_{0}^{rad}\leq\mathfrak{z}\}
is independent of the angular component of the trajectory, allowing us to bound from above its probability with the tools developed in Section
4
, while the treatment of the second event
\{T_{0}^{ang}\leq\mathfrak{z}<T_{0}^{rad}\}
will follow from standard results on Brownian motion and the control of
I(\mathfrak{z})
. The following result allows us to bound the second term on the right-hand side of (
26
):

Proposition 37
.

Define
J(r):=\cosech^{-2}(\beta r)
. Then,

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{ang}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z}\leq T^{rad}_{0})\,\leq\,4\Phi\Big({-}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}J(\widehat{r}_{0})}}\Big).

where
\Phi
stands for the error function. Furthermore,

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{ang}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma\big)d\sigma.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\cdot\mid\{r_{u}\}_{u\geq 0})
the law of the angular process given a realization of its radial component. Given such a realization we already know that the trajectory
\{\theta_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
is equal in law to that of the time-changed Brownian motion
\{B_{I(u)}+\theta_{0}\}_{u\geq 0}
, for which
T^{ang}_{0}
is equal to the exit time of
B_{I(u)}
from
[-\widehat{\theta}_{0},\widehat{\theta}_{0}]
. Since the exit time of a Brownian motion is well known, we proceed as in Section
3
using the reflection principle to obtain

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{ang}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z}\,|\{r_{u}\}_{u\geq 0})\leq 4\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(B_{I(\mathfrak{z})}\leq-\widehat{\theta}_{0}\mid\{r_{u}\}_{u\geq 0})=4\Phi\Big({-}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sqrt{I(\mathfrak{z})}}\Big)

where the factor 4 is obtained since the probability of exiting by hitting one of the angles is twice the probability of hitting any of the two angles, which is twice the probability of hitting one fixed angle. Taking expectations with respect to the law of
\{r_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
we obtain

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{ang}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z}\leq T^{rad}_{0})\leq 4\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}\Big(\Phi\Big({-}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sqrt{I(\mathfrak{z})}}\Big){\bf 1}_{\{\mathfrak{z}\leq T^{rad}_{0}\}}\Big),

where the expression within the expectation depends on the radial movement alone. To apply this last bound observe that for any realization of
\{r_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
such that
T^{rad}_{0}\geq\mathfrak{z}
we have that
\inf_{0\leq u\leq\mathfrak{z}}r_{u}\geq\widehat{r}_{0}
, so
I(\mathfrak{z})\leq\mathfrak{z}J(\widehat{r}_{0})
, which gives the first part of the proposition’s statement. For the second part of the proposition we can make the same computation as before without taking into account the event
\{T^{rad}_{0}\geq\mathfrak{z}\}
, giving

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{ang}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})\,\leq\,4\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}\Big(\Phi\Big({-}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sqrt{I(\mathfrak{z})}}\Big)\Big)\,=\,-4\int_{0}^{\infty}\Phi\Big({-}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}\Big)d\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma\big),

and the result follows after using integration by parts, since
\frac{d}{d\sigma}\Phi(-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sqrt{\sigma}})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}
.
∎

On a high level, in the proposition above, the bound for
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{ang}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z}<T^{rad}_{0})
will be useful as long as
\mathfrak{z}=O(1)
since in this case the inequality
\mathfrak{z}J(\widehat{r}_{0})\leq I(\mathfrak{z})
is not too loose. However, when
\mathfrak{z}=\omega(1)
we need to control this term using the second part of Proposition
37
, which relies heavily on bounding probabilities of the form
\mathbb{P}(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)
. To provide these bounds observe that when
\inf_{0\leq u\leq\mathfrak{z}}r_{u}
is not too close to zero the drift function is almost constant so the trajectories of
\{r_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
resemble those of a Brownian motion with drift
\tfrac{\alpha}{2}
(with a reflecting boundary at
R
). Even further, on the event where
\inf_{0\leq u\leq\mathfrak{z}}r_{u}
is not too close to zero we also have

I(\mathfrak{z})\approx\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-2\beta r_{u}}du,

(27)

where the integral appearing above on the right-hand side has been widely studied in the case when
\{r_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
is an (unbounded) Brownian motion with drift (see
[
31
,
41
,
16
,
15
]
), revealing that their distributions are heavy-tailed with the tail exponent given analytically in terms of
\beta
and the drift of
\{r_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
. In order to make use of known results we must first address the change in behavior arising from the reflecting boundary of our process. To do so, we consider an auxiliary process
\{\widetilde{r}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
akin to the one introduced in the proof of Proposition
20
:

•

\{\widetilde{r}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
begins at
r_{0}
and evolves according to
\Delta_{rad}
up until hitting
R
.

•

Every time
\{\widetilde{r}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
hits
R
, it is immediately restarted at
R-1
and continues to evolve as before.

It is not hard to see that
\{\widetilde{r}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}

is stochastically dominated by
\{r_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
, in the sense that the radius of the auxiliary process is always smaller. Thus, in particular,
I(\mathfrak{z})\leq\widetilde{I}(\mathfrak{z}):=\int_{0}^{s}\cosech^{2}(\beta\widetilde{r}_{u})du
, and hence it will be enough to bound probabilities of the form
\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{I}(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)
from above. Now, from the definition of
\widetilde{r}_{u}
, it is natural to use the subsequent hitting times of
R
, say
0<T_{R}^{(1)}<T_{R}^{(2)}<\ldots
, to divide the trajectory of
\{\widetilde{r}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
into excursions from
R-1
to
R
(or from
r_{0}
to
R
, in the case of the first one), giving

\widetilde{I}(\mathfrak{z})\,\leq\,\int_{0}^{T^{(1)}_{R}}\cosech^{2}(\beta\widetilde{r}_{u})du\,+\,\sum_{i=1}^{M(\mathfrak{z})}\int_{T^{(i)}_{R}}^{T^{(i+1)}_{R}}\cosech^{2}(\beta\widetilde{r}_{u})du,

where
M(\mathfrak{z})
is a random variable equal to the amount of times
\{\widetilde{r}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
has hit
R
by time
\mathfrak{z}
. Let
\{\widetilde{r}^{(0)}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0},\{\widetilde{r}^{(1)}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0},\{\widetilde{r}^{(2)}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0},\ldots
be independent random diffusion processes (that will correspond to the different excursions), each evolving on
(0,\infty)
according to the generator
\Delta_{rad}
without the reflecting boundary at
R
, and such that
\{\widetilde{r}^{(0)}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
starts at
r_{0}
while the rest of the
\{\widetilde{r}^{(i)}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
start at
R-1
. From the definition of the auxiliary process it follows that within each time interval of the form
[T^{(i)}_{R},T^{(i+1)}_{R}]
(or
[0,T^{(1)}_{R}]
in the case of the first excursion) the trajectory of
\{\widetilde{r}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
is equal to the one of
\{\widetilde{r}^{(i)}_{u^{\prime}}\}_{u^{\prime}\geq 0}
for
u^{\prime}\leq T^{(i+1)}_{R}-T^{(i)}_{R}
. Hence,

\widetilde{I}(\mathfrak{z})\,\leq\,\int_{0}^{T^{(1)}_{R}}\cosech^{2}(\beta\widetilde{r}^{(0)}_{u})du\,+\,\sum_{i=1}^{M(\mathfrak{z})}\int_{0}^{T^{(i+1)}_{R}-T^{(i)}_{R}}\cosech^{2}(\beta\widetilde{r}^{(i)}_{u})du\,\leq\,\sum_{i=0}^{M(\mathfrak{z})}\widetilde{I}^{(i)},

where for
i=0,\ldots,M(\mathfrak{z})

\widetilde{I}^{(i)}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\cosech^{2}(\beta\widetilde{r}^{(i)}_{u})du.

Observe that the bound on
\widetilde{I}(\mathfrak{z})
involves a sum of
M(\mathfrak{z})+1
random variables. Intuitively speaking the number of times the process hits
R
should be proportional to
\mathfrak{z}
, so we introduce an auxiliary parameter
v_{0}>0
depending on
x_{0}
, to be fixed later, so that the event
M(\mathfrak{z})>\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil
occurs with a very small probability. Using this new parameter we obtain

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)\,\leq\,\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(M(\mathfrak{z})>\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\big)+\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(\widetilde{I}^{(0)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma\big)+\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}\widetilde{I}^{(i)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma\Big).

(28)

The advantage of the bound above is that it involves the sum of independent random variables
\widetilde{I}^{(i)}
which, aside from
\widetilde{I}^{(0)}
, are also identically distributed. The bound also gives valuable intuition regarding the detection of the target: roughly speaking, in order for detection to take place either
x_{0}
must be sufficiently close to the origin so that the angular variance coming from
\widetilde{I}^{(0)}
becomes significant, or
\mathfrak{z}
must be large enough so that many excursions starting near the boundary occur, allowing for the contribution
\sum_{i=1}^{M(\mathfrak{z})}\widetilde{I}^{(i)}
to be large enough. As mentioned above (the discussion following (
27
)), the distributions of the
\widetilde{I}^{(i)}
variables are heavy-tailed. In order to control their sum we make use of the following lemma, whose proof mimics closely what was done in
[
35
]
: since our result is slightly more precise than the one in
[
35
]
, we provide it in the appendix for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 38
.

Let
S_{m}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z_{i}
where
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of i.i.d. absolutely continuous random variables taking values in
[1,\infty)
such that there are
V,\gamma>0
for which for all
x\geq 0

1-F_{Z_{i}}(x)=\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}\geq x)\leq Vx^{-\gamma}.

Then, there are
\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{L}>0
depending on
V
,
\gamma
and
\mathbb{E}(Z_{1})
(if it exists) alone such that:

•

If
\gamma<1
and
L>\mathfrak{L}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(S_{m}\geq Lm^{\frac{1}{\gamma}})\leq\mathfrak{C}L^{-\gamma}
.

•

If
\gamma=1
and
L>\mathfrak{L}
, then
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(S_{m}\geq Lm\log(m))\leq\Big(\frac{\mathfrak{C}}{L\log(m)}\Big)^{1-\frac{\mathfrak{L}}{L}}
.

•

If
\gamma>1
and
L>\mathfrak{L}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(S_{m}\geq Lm)\,\leq\,\mathfrak{C}L^{-\gamma}m^{-((\gamma-1)\wedge\frac{\gamma}{2})}
.

We can now prove the following result, which will allow us to control
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)
:

Proposition 39
.

For any
0<c<1
there are
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}
large depending on
\alpha
and
\beta
alone, such that for any
\mathfrak{z}\geq c
,
v_{0}>\frac{4}{c}
, and
x_{0}\in B_{O}(R)
with
r_{0}>1
, defining
\sigma_{0}
as

\sigma_{0}:=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}e^{-2\beta R}(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})^{1\vee\frac{2\beta}{\alpha}},&\text{if $\alpha\neq 2\beta$,}\\
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}e^{-2\beta R}v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z}),&\text{if $\alpha=2\beta$,}\end{cases}

the following statements hold for all
\sigma>\sigma_{0}
and all
R
sufficiently large:

(i)

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(M(\mathfrak{z})>\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil)\leq e^{-\frac{1}{16}v_{0}^{2}\mathfrak{z}}
,

(ii)

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\widetilde{I}^{(0)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma)\leq\frac{\log(\tanh(\alpha r_{0}/2))}{\log(\tanh(\alpha/2))}+\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}
, and

(iii)

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}\widetilde{I}^{(i)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma\big)\leq 2v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\frac{\log(\tanh(\alpha((R-1)/2))}{\log(\tanh(\alpha/2))}+\big(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}\big)^{1-\mathfrak{e}}

where

\mathfrak{e}:=\begin{cases}0,&\mbox{ if }\alpha\neq 2\beta,\\[3.0pt]
\frac{1}{\sigma e^{2\beta R}}\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z}),&\mbox{ if }\alpha=2\beta.\end{cases}

Proof.

We begin with the upper bound for the term
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(M(\mathfrak{z})>\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil)
. By definition, the event
\{M(\mathfrak{z})>\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\}
is equal to
\{T^{(\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil)}_{R}\leq\mathfrak{z}\}
. Writing
T^{(\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil)}_{R}=T^{(1)}_{R}+\sum_{i=2}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}(T^{(i)}_{R}-T^{(i-1)}_{R})
, by Markov’s inequality, for any
\lambda>0
we deduce

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(M(\mathfrak{z})>\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\big)\,\leq\,\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=2}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}(T^{(i)}_{R}-T^{(i-1)}_{R})\leq\mathfrak{z}\Big)\,\leq\,e^{\lambda\mathfrak{z}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}\Big(\exp\big({-}\lambda\sum_{i=2}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}(T^{(i)}_{R}-T^{(i-1)}_{R})\big)\Big)

where the differences
T^{(i)}_{R}-T^{(i-1)}_{R}
are i.i.d. random variables which indicate the time it takes for excursion
\widetilde{r}^{(i-1)}
starting at
R-1
to reach
R
. Since each
\{\widetilde{r}^{(i)}_{u}\}_{u\geq 0}
is stochastically bounded from below by a Brownian motion with constant drift
\tfrac{\alpha}{2}
, we can use Formula 2.0.1 in
[
11
]
to obtain

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(M(\mathfrak{z})>\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\big)\,\leq\,e^{\lambda\mathfrak{z}}\mathbb{E}\big(e^{-\lambda(T^{(2)}_{R}-T^{(1)}_{R})}\big)^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil-1}\,\leq\,e^{\lambda\mathfrak{z}}\big(e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4}+2\lambda}}\big)^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil-1}.

The exponent
\lambda\mathfrak{z}+(\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil-1)(\frac{\alpha}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4}+2\lambda})
appearing in the last term is minimized as a function of
\lambda
when
\mathfrak{z}\sqrt{\tfrac{\alpha^{2}}{4}+2\lambda}=\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil-1
, which defines a positive
\lambda
since
v_{0}\mathfrak{z}>4
and
\alpha,c<1
, giving an expression for the exponent of the right-hand side of the form

-\frac{1}{2\mathfrak{z}}(\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil-1)^{2}-\frac{1}{8}\alpha^{2}\mathfrak{z}+\frac{1}{2}\alpha(\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil-1)\leq\frac{1}{2}(\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil-1)\Big(\alpha-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{z}}(\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil-1)\Big)\,\leq\,-\frac{1}{16}\,v_{0}^{2}\mathfrak{z}

where in the last inequality we used that
\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil-1\geq\frac{1}{2}v_{0}\mathfrak{z}
and that
\alpha-\frac{1}{2}v_{0}\leq-\frac{1}{4}v_{0}
. This proves the bound in (
i
). In order to control the probabilities appearing in (
ii
) and (
iii
) it is convenient from the point of view of computations to work under the assumption that the
\widetilde{r}^{(i)}
processes never get too close to
0
. To do so observe that by our assumption of
r_{0}>1
, all the
\widetilde{r}^{(i)}
start in
(1,\infty)
, and denote by
\tau^{(i)}_{1}
the hitting time of radius
1
by
\widetilde{r}^{(i)}
. Observe that

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\widetilde{I}^{(0)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma)\,\leq\,\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\tau_{1}^{(0)}<\infty)+\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\widetilde{I}^{(0)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma,\tau^{(0)}_{1}=\infty).

Using Part (
iii
) from Lemma
14
, with
{\bf y}_{0}:=1
and
Y:=R
, we obtain

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\tau_{1}^{(0)}<\infty)=\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\tau_{1}^{(0)}<T_{R})=\frac{\log(\tanh(\alpha r_{0}/2))}{\log(\tanh(\alpha/2))},

which gives the first term of the bound for
R
(and thus
n
) sufficiently large. For the second term, observe that on the event
\tau^{(0)}_{1}=\infty
we have
\widetilde{r}^{(0)}_{u}>1
for all
u\geq 0
so there is some constant
C
depending on
\beta
alone such that for all
u\geq 0
, we have
\cosech^{2}(\beta\widetilde{r}^{(0)}_{u})\leq Ce^{-2\beta\widetilde{r}^{(0)}_{u}}
and hence

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\widetilde{I}^{(0)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma,\tau^{(0)}_{1}=\infty)\,\leq\,\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(C\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-2\beta\widetilde{r}^{(0)}_{u}}du\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma\Big).

Now, notice that
\{\widetilde{r}_{u}^{(0)}-r_{0}\}_{u\geq 0}
is stochastically bounded from below by
X_{u}
, a Brownian motion with constant drift
\tfrac{\alpha}{2}
so we can bound the integral within the last term from above by
\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-2\beta(X_{u}+r_{0})}du
. This particular functional of Brownian motion was studied in
[
15
, Proposition 4.4.4]
, where it was shown that

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-2\beta X_{u}}du\,=\,W,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }W\text{ is a positive r.v.~with }\hskip 10.00002ptf_{W}(x):=\frac{(2\beta^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2\beta})}x^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}-1}e^{-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{x}}

that is,
W
is distributed according to an inverse gamma distribution with parameters
\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}
and
2\beta^{2}
. From the density of the variable
W
above the only relevant feature for our purposes is its heavy tail: in order to ease calculations we will bound
W
stochastically from above by a random variable
Z
following a Pareto distribution with a sufficiently large scale
\omega
depending on
\alpha
and
\beta
alone (in particular we will assume
\omega>1
), and shape
\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}
. That is,
W\preccurlyeq Z
with

f_{Z}(x)\,=\,\frac{2\beta}{\alpha\omega}\Big(\frac{\omega}{x}\Big)^{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}+1}{\bf 1}_{(\omega,\infty)}(x).

Using this auxiliary variable we readily obtain

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(C\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-2\beta\widetilde{r}^{(0)}_{u}}du\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\big(Z\geq\tfrac{\sigma}{2C}e^{2\beta r_{0}}\big)\leq\Big(\frac{\sigma e^{2\beta r_{0}}}{2C\omega}\Big)^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}},

giving the last term of (
ii
). To obtain the bound in (
iii
), define the event
\mathcal{E}:=\{\exists 1\leq i\leq\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil,\,\tau_{1}^{(i)}<\infty\}
where
\tau_{1}^{(i)}
was defined previously as the hitting time of
r=1
on the
i
-th excursion. By the same analysis as in the previous paragraph, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}\widetilde{I}^{(i)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\,\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\mathcal{E})+\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}\widetilde{I}^{(i)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma,\,\overline{\mathcal{E}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\,\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\tau_{1}^{(1)}<\infty)+\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(C\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-2\beta\widetilde{r}^{(i)}_{u}}du\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\,\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\tau_{1}^{(1)}<\infty)+\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}Z^{(i)}\geq\tfrac{\sigma}{2C}e^{2\beta(R-1)}\Big)

where in this case we have used that each
\widetilde{r}^{(i)}
begins at
R-1
and the
Z^{(i)}
’s stand for i.i.d. Pareto random variables with scale
\omega
and shape
\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}
as in the previous case. For the first term we use the same treatment as with
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\tau_{1}^{(0)}<\infty)
to obtain

\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(\tau_{1}^{(1)}<\infty)\,\leq\,2v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\frac{\log(\tanh(\alpha(R-1)/2))}{\log(\tanh(\alpha/2))}

where we have used that in the first excursion the starting radius is
R-1
, and that
v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\geq 4
. For the second term recall that the
Z^{(i)}
variables are bounded from below by
\omega\geq 1
, and that
1-F_{Z}(x)=(\frac{\omega}{x})^{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}
for any
x\geq\omega
. Hence, we can apply Lemma
38
with
m:=\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil
and
S_{m}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}Z^{(i)}
depending on the value of
\gamma:=\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}
as follows:

•

If
\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}<1
, then we can take
L:=\tfrac{\sigma}{2C}\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil^{-\frac{2\beta}{\alpha}}e^{2\beta(R-1)}
in the lemma so that

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}Z^{(i)}\geq\tfrac{\sigma}{2C}e^{2\beta(R-1)}\Big)=\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(S_{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}\geq L\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil^{\frac{2\beta}{\alpha}}\Big)\leq\mathfrak{C}L^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}

as soon as
L>\mathfrak{L}
for some
\mathfrak{L}
and
\mathfrak{C}
depending on
\alpha,\beta
and
\omega
alone. Observing that
L^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}=(2C)^{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}e^{-\alpha(R-1)}
and that the condition
L>\mathfrak{L}
is equivalent to

\sigma>2\mathfrak{L}C\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil^{\frac{2\beta}{\alpha}}e^{-2\beta(R-1)}
, the result follows by defining
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
accordingly.

•

If
\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}=1
we can take
L:=\tfrac{\sigma}{2C}(\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\log\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil)^{-1}e^{2\beta(R-1)}
in the lemma so that

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}Z^{(i)}\geq\tfrac{\sigma}{2C}e^{2\beta(R-1)}\Big)=\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(S_{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}\geq L\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\log\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\big)\leq\Big(\frac{\mathfrak{C}}{L\log\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}\Big)^{1-\frac{\mathfrak{L}}{L}}

as soon as
L>\mathfrak{L}
for some
\mathfrak{L}
and
\mathfrak{C}
depending on
\alpha,\beta
and
\omega
alone, which is satisfied since by hypothesis
\sigma>\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}e^{-2\beta R}v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})
and we can choose
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}>2Ce^{2\beta}\mathfrak{L}
. Since
\alpha=2\beta
, we have
\tfrac{\mathfrak{C}}{L\log\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}=\frac{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}{\sigma}e^{-\alpha R}\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil
for
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}:=2C\mathfrak{C}e^{\alpha}
, giving the result.

•

Finally, assume that
\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}>1
and take
L:=\tfrac{\sigma}{2C\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}e^{2\beta(R-1)}
in the lemma so that

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}Z^{(i)}\geq\tfrac{\sigma}{2C}e^{2\beta(R-1)}\Big)=\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(S_{\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil}\geq L\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil\big)\leq\mathfrak{C}L^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil^{-(\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}-1\wedge\frac{\alpha}{4\beta})}

as soon as
L>\mathfrak{L}
for some
\mathfrak{L}
and
\mathfrak{C}
depending on
\alpha,\beta
and
\omega
alone. The condition follows directly from the hypothesis
\sigma>\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}e^{-2\beta R}(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})
for
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}
adequately chosen, and the bound in (
iii
) is obtained from the previous inequality by noticing that
L^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}=(2C\lceil v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\rceil)^{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}e^{-\alpha(R-1)}
and defining
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
accordingly.

∎

5.2.1
The case
\mathfrak{z}
small

In this subsection we obtain upper bound for
\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})
under the assumption of
\beta\leq 1/2
and
\mathfrak{z}=O(1)
as required by Theorem
29
. Since for
\beta=1/2
we always have
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
and the next subsection deals with all such values, here we even assume
\beta<1/2
. The main result in this section is the following:

Proposition 40
.

Let
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
. If
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
is as defined in Theorem
29
and
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega((e^{\beta R}/n)^{2})\cap O(1)
, then

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})\;=\;O\big(ne^{-\beta R}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}\big).

Proof.

Notice first that for any
C^{\prime}>0
, Lemma
6
gives
\mu(B_{O}(C^{\prime}))=O(ne^{-\alpha R})=o(1)
so the contribution of points in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}\cap B_{O}(C^{\prime})
to the upper bound is
o(ne^{-\beta R}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}})
from our assumption
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega((e^{2\beta R}/n)^{2})
, and hence such points can be neglected. Take then
C^{\prime}>0
large (to be fixed later) so we only need to consider particles initially located at points with radial component in
[C^{\prime},R]
. For such points we follow the proof argument described in the previous section, where we bound the detection time of the target by the exit time of the box

\mathcal{B}(x_{0})
for
\widehat{\theta}_{0}
and
\widehat{r}_{0}
given in the next sentence, allowing us to bound
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})
as in (
26
). More in detail, given
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
we construct the box
\mathcal{B}(x_{0})
by choosing

\widehat{\theta}_{0}:=\tfrac{1}{3}(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{r}_{0}:=r_{0}-\log\big(1+(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))e^{\beta r_{0}}\big).

Notice first that
\widehat{\theta}_{0}>0
,
\widehat{r}_{0}<r_{0}
, and assuming
C^{\prime}>13
we also have
\widehat{r}_{0}>\frac{1}{3}r_{0}
: indeed, this last statement is equivalent to

e^{\frac{2}{3}r_{0}}>1+(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))e^{\beta r_{0}}

which is satisfied for
r_{0}>2/(\frac{2}{3}-\beta)+1\geq 13
since
\beta\leq 1/2
and
|\theta_{0}|\leq\pi
. The previous inequalities show that the box is well defined and contain the point
(r_{0},\theta_{0})
, but we still need to prove that
\mathcal{B}(x_{0})\subseteq\overline{B}_{Q}(R)
. To do so
assume, without loss of generality, that
\theta_{0}>0
and
observe that it is enough to show that the upper-left corner of the box,
(\widehat{r}_{0},\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})
is in
\overline{B}_{Q}(R)
, or equivalently, that
\phi(\widehat{r}_{0})<\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0}=\frac{2}{3}(\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0}))+\phi(r_{0})
. Using that
\widehat{r}_{0}<r_{0}
and Part (
i
) of Lemma
8
we obtain that

\displaystyle\cos(\phi(r_{0}))-\cos(\phi(\widehat{r}_{0}))

\displaystyle=(\tanh(\tfrac{r_{0}}{2})-\tanh(\tfrac{\widehat{r}_{0}}{2}))\coth(R)=\coth(R)\frac{2(e^{r_{0}}-e^{\widehat{r}_{0}})}{(e^{r_{0}}+1)(e^{\widehat{r}_{0}}+1)}

\displaystyle\leq 4(e^{-\widehat{r}_{0}}-e^{-r_{0}})=4e^{(\beta-1)r_{0}}(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0})).

Moreover, for
C^{\prime}
sufficiently large, since
\cos(x)-\cos(x^{\prime})=2\sin(\frac{1}{2}(x+x^{\prime}))\sin(\frac{1}{2}(x-x^{\prime}))
,

\cos(\phi(r_{0}))-\cos(\phi(\widehat{r}_{0}))\geq 2\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\phi(r_{0}))\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}(\phi(\widehat{r}_{0})-\phi(r_{0})))\geq\tfrac{1}{8}\phi(r_{0})(\phi(\widehat{r}_{0})-\phi(r_{0})).

Using Part (
v
) of Lemma
8
we have
\phi(r_{0})\geq e^{-\frac{r_{0}}{2}}
for
r_{0}>C^{\prime}
so we conclude that

\phi(\widehat{r}_{0})-\phi(r_{0})\,\leq\,32e^{(\beta-\frac{1}{2})r_{0}}(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))

and since
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
, taking
C^{\prime}
sufficiently large the last term is bounded by
\frac{2}{3}(\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0}))
which proves that
\mathcal{B}(x_{0})\subseteq\overline{B}_{Q}(R)
for all such
r_{0}
.

Now, we follow the proof strategy presented in the previous section by splitting the detection probability as in (
26
) and using the first bound in Proposition
37
, which by setting
\Omega:=\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}\cap\overline{B}_{O}(C^{\prime})
and recalling that
J(\widehat{r}_{0}):=\cosech^{2}(\beta\widehat{r}_{0})
, gives

\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})\leq\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{0}^{rad}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})+\int_{\Omega}4\Phi\Big({-}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}J(\widehat{r}_{0})}}\Big)d\mu(x_{0})

(29)

where
T_{0}^{rad}
is the hitting time of
\widehat{r}_{0}
. For the first term on the right of (
29
) we can stochastically dominate from above the trajectory of
\{r_{s}\}_{s\geq 0}
by that of a simple Brownian motion with a reflecting barrier at
R
(thus removing the drift towards the boundary) which decreases the hitting time of
\widehat{r}_{0}
. Since hitting
\widehat{r}_{0}
implies moving outside of an interval of length
2(r_{0}-\widehat{r}_{0})
, by the reflection principle, we obtain

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{0}^{rad}\leq\mathfrak{z})\,\leq\,4\Phi\Big({-}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}}(r_{0}-\widehat{r}_{0})\Big)\,=\,4\Phi\Big({-}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}}\log\Big(1+\frac{|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0})}{e^{-\beta r_{0}}}\Big)\Big).

For the term on the right we have

\int_{\phi(r_{0})+\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta r_{0}}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{0}^{rad}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\theta_{0}=4\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta r_{0}}\int_{1}^{\infty}\Phi\Big({-}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}}\log(1+\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}w)\Big)dw

but since the function
x\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}\log(1+\sqrt{x}w)
is decreasing and
\mathfrak{z}=O(1)
we deduce that the term on the right-hand side is
O(\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta R})
, so

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{0}^{rad}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})

\displaystyle=\;O\Big(n\int_{C^{\prime}}^{R}\int_{\phi(r_{0})+\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta r_{0}}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{0}^{rad}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\theta_{0}e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}\Big)

\displaystyle=\;O\Big(n\int_{C^{\prime}}^{R}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta r_{0}}e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}\Big)=O\big(n\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta R}\big)

where in the last equality we have used that
\beta<\frac{1}{2}<\alpha
. Next, we must bound the second term on the right of (
29
), for which we observe first that since
\widehat{r}_{0}>\frac{1}{3}r_{0}
and assuming that
C^{\prime}>0
is large we have
\sinh(\beta\widehat{r}_{0})>\frac{1}{4}e^{\beta\widehat{r}_{0}}
so that

{-}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}J(\widehat{r}_{0})}}=\frac{\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0})}{3\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}J(\widehat{r}_{0})}}\leq{-}\frac{4(\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0}))}{3\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta r_{0}}}\Big(1+\frac{\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0})}{e^{-\beta r_{0}}}\Big)^{-\beta}.

Evaluating at
\Phi(\cdot)
, integrating over
\Omega
and using the change of variables
w:=\frac{\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0})}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta r_{0}}}
gives

\int_{\Omega}\Phi\Big({-}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}J(\widehat{r}_{0})}}\Big)d\mu(x_{0})\;=\;O\Big(n\int_{C^{\prime}}^{R}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta r_{0}}\Big(\int_{1}^{\infty}\Phi\Big({-}\frac{4w}{3(1+\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}w)^{\beta}}\Big)dw\Big)e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}\Big)

but since
\mathfrak{z}=O(1)
we have
\int_{1}^{\infty}\Phi(-\tfrac{4w}{3(1+\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}w)^{\beta}})dw=O(1)
(it is finite since
\beta<1/2
) and hence

\int_{\Omega}\Phi\Big({-}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}J(\widehat{r}_{0})}}\Big)d\mu(x_{0})\,=\,O\Big(n\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}\int_{C^{\prime}}^{R}e^{-\beta r_{0}+\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}\Big)\,=\,O\big(n\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}}e^{-\beta R}\big)

where again we used that
\beta<\frac{1}{2}<\alpha
in the last equality. ∎

5.2.2
The case
\mathfrak{z}
large

In this subsection we will prove the upper bounds in Theorem
30
for both
\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})
as well as for
\sup_{x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})
, where for convenience we use the shorthand notation
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}=\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}(\kappa_{A})
that will be used throughout this section. Recall that we may assume
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
for this theorem. We handle this case with the help of Propositions
37
and
39
, as well as with the results obtained in Section
4
.
The main result in this section is the following, which directly implies the corresponding upper bounds of Theorem
30
:

Proposition 41
.

Let
\mathcal{D}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
be as in Theorem
30
. Then there is a
C>0
independent of
\mathfrak{z}
and
n
such that for
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
satisfying the conditions of Theorem
30
, fixing
\kappa_{A}:=C
gives

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})\;=\;O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}).

Furthermore, for
\kappa_{A}\geq C
and under the additional assumption
\mathfrak{z}=\omega(1)
we have

\sup_{x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\begin{cases}e^{-\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{2})},&\text{ if $\alpha\geq 2\beta$,}\\[2.0pt]
O(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha(\frac{1}{\beta}\vee 2)}),&\text{ if $\alpha<2\beta$.}\end{cases}

We begin by introducing some notation and deriving a few facts that will come in handy throughout this section. The points in
\partial\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
with a positive angle define a curve in polar coordinates which can be parameterized by the radius as
(r,\gamma(r))
with
\gamma(r):=\max\{\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})},\phi(r)+\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r}\}
, and where
r
takes values between
r^{\prime}
and
R
, for
r^{\prime}
being the solution of (see Figure
3
(b) for a depiction of
r^{\prime}
)

\phi(r^{\prime})+\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r^{\prime}}=\pi.

(30)

Observing that
\gamma
is the maximum between two functions, a major role will be played by the intersecting point
(r^{\prime\prime},\gamma(r^{\prime\prime}))
which satisfies (see Figure
3
(b) for a depiction of
r^{\prime\prime}
)

\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=\phi(r^{\prime\prime})+\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r^{\prime\prime}}.

(31)

Next we derive several inequalities that will be useful in the proof of Proposition
41
:

Fact 42
.

If the constant
C>0
appearing in the statement of Proposition
41
is sufficiently large, then under the assumption
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
the following hold:

(i)

\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=\Omega(\phi(R))
and for all
0<r_{0}\leq R
,
e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r_{0}}=\Omega(\phi(r_{0}))
. In particular, for any
C^{\prime}>0
by taking
C
large we have
\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\geq C^{\prime}\phi(R)
and
\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r_{0}}\geq C^{\prime}\phi(r_{0})
.

(ii)

(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})^{-1}\log(\frac{1}{\pi}\kappa_{R})\leq r^{\prime}\leq(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})^{-1}\log(\frac{2}{\pi}\kappa_{R})
.

(iii)

e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r^{\prime\prime}}=\Theta(\tfrac{\kappa_{A}}{\kappa_{R}}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
.

(iv)

\gamma(r)-\phi(r)
is minimized at
r^{\prime\prime}
, and in particular
\gamma(r)-\phi(r)\geq(\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r}\vee\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
for all
r^{\prime}\leq r\leq R
.

(v)

For any fixed value of
\kappa_{A}
and
\kappa_{R}
,
\displaystyle\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}(\gamma(r)-\phi(r))e^{-\alpha(R-r)}dr=O(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}).

Proof.

The first part follows directly from Part (
v
) of Lemma
8
,
the second one from the definition of
r^{\prime}
and the fact that
0\leq\phi(\cdot)\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
, and
the third part follows from (
i
) and the definition of
r^{\prime\prime}
. To prove part (
iv
) observe that in
[r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime}]
the function
\gamma(r)-\phi(r)=\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r}
is decreasing while in
[r^{\prime\prime},R]
it is increasing (since
\gamma
is constant and
\phi
is decreasing) so the function is minimized at
r^{\prime\prime}
. The inequality
\gamma(r)-\phi(r)\geq(\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r}\vee\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
follows from

\gamma(r^{\prime\prime})-\phi(r^{\prime\prime})=\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r^{\prime\prime}}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}(\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r^{\prime\prime}}+\phi(r^{\prime\prime}))=\tfrac{1}{2}(\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}+\phi(R))\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}.

To prove (
v
) we fix
\kappa_{A}=C_{A}
and
\kappa_{R}=C_{R}
equal to some constants and split the range of integration into two intervals, specifically
[r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime}]
and
[r^{\prime\prime},R]
. For the first one we have
\gamma(r)=\phi(r)+C_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r}
and since
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
, using (
iii
), we get

\int_{r^{\prime}}^{r^{\prime\prime}}(\gamma(r)-\phi(r))e^{-\alpha(R-r)}dr=O\big(e^{-\alpha R}e^{(\alpha-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r^{\prime\prime})}\big)=O\big(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}e^{-\alpha(R-r^{\prime\prime})}\big)=O(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}).

To conclude the proof of (
v
) observe that for the second interval (
i
) gives

\int_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{R}(\gamma(r)-\phi(r))e^{-\alpha(R-r)}dr=O(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}).

∎

Following the strategy presented at the beginning of Section
5.2
, for each
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
with
\theta_{0}>0
(the case
\theta_{0}<0
is analogous) we define a box
\mathcal{B}(x_{0})\subseteq\overline{B}_{Q}(R)
where this time we set (see Figure
3
(c) for an illustration of
\mathcal{B}(x_{0})
)

\widehat{\theta}_{0}=\tfrac{2}{3}(\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0}))\hskip 20.00003pt\text{ and }\hskip 20.00003pt\phi(\widehat{r}_{0})=\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0}.

(32)

Since by Part (
ii
) of Lemma
8
the function
\phi(\cdot)
is decreasing, it can be easily checked that
x_{0}\in\mathcal{B}(x_{0})\subseteq\overline{B}_{Q}(R)
so in order to detect the target, a particle must first exit its respective box.
In particular, by (
26
) and the second bound in Proposition
37
, we obtain

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T_{det}\leq\mathfrak{z})\,\leq\,\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{rad}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)d\sigma.

(33)

It suffices thus to bound the right-hand side. We first address the term
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{rad}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})
, which depends on the radial trajectory of the particle alone, and hence we can make use of the results obtained in Section
4
.

Proposition 43
.

Under the conditions of Proposition
41
we have:

•

If
\kappa_{A}=C
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{rad}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
.

•

If
\kappa_{A}\geq C
and
\mathfrak{z}=\omega(1)
, then

\sup_{x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{rad}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})=\begin{cases}e^{-\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{2})},&\text{ if $\alpha\geq 2\beta$,}\\[2.0pt]
O(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha(\frac{1}{\beta}\vee 2)}),&\text{ if $\alpha<2\beta$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Throughout this proof, let
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
be such that
\theta_{0}>0
(the case
\theta_{0}<0
can be dealt with analogously).
As defined in Part (
iii
) of Lemma
14
, with
y=r_{0}
,
{\bf y}_{0}=\widehat{r}_{0}
and
Y=R
, let

G_{\widehat{r}_{0}}(r_{0}):=\frac{g(r_{0})}{g(\widehat{r}_{0})},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptg(r):=\log(\tanh(\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha R)/\tanh(\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha r))

Using the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
19
in Section
4
, bounding the term
\mathbb{P}_{R}(T_{\widehat{r}_{0}}\leq\mathfrak{z})
analogously to what was done in (
16
), we have

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{rad}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})\;\leq\;\mathbb{P}_{r_{0}}(T_{\widehat{r}_{0}}<T_{R})+\mathbb{P}_{R}(T_{\widehat{r}_{0}}\leq\mathfrak{z})\;=\;G_{\widehat{r}_{0}}(r_{0})+O(\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha(R-\widehat{r}_{0})}).

(34)

To bound from above the numerator of
G_{\widehat{r}_{0}}(r_{0})
, note that the largest possible value of
\widehat{r}_{0}
among points in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
is obtained at
r_{0}=R
and
\theta_{0}=\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
so it follows from the definition of
\widehat{r}_{0}
,
\widehat{\theta}_{0}
,
\phi(\cdot)
, and Part (
i
) in Fact
42
, that

\phi(\widehat{r}_{0})\geq\phi(R)+\tfrac{1}{3}(\theta_{0}-\phi(R))=\phi(R)+\tfrac{1}{3}\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=\Omega(\kappa_{A}e^{-R/2}).

Also note that
\phi(\widehat{r}_{0})=O(e^{-\widehat{r}_{0}/2})
, so taking
\kappa_{A}
larger than a fixed constant gives
R-\widehat{r}_{0}=\Omega(1)
, and hence

\log\Big(\frac{\tanh(\alpha R/2)}{\tanh(\alpha\widehat{r}_{0}/2)}\Big)\;=\;\log\Big(1+\frac{2-2e^{\alpha(\widehat{r}_{0}-R)}}{(e^{-\alpha R}+1)(e^{\alpha\widehat{r}_{0}}-1)}\Big)\,=\,\log\big(1+O(e^{-\alpha\widehat{r}_{0}})\big)=O(e^{-\alpha\widehat{r}_{0}}).

From Part (
ii
) in Fact
42
it follows that
r^{\prime}=\Omega(\log\kappa_{R})=\Omega(1)
, and since
r_{0}\geq r^{\prime}
(because
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
) we can argue as in the proof of Fact
17
that
g(r_{0})=O(e^{-\alpha r_{0}})
, and hence combining with the previous bound we get
G_{\widehat{r}_{0}}(r_{0})=O(e^{-\alpha(r_{0}-\widehat{r}_{0})})
. Notice that both terms bounding
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{rad}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})
in (
34
) involve a factor
e^{\alpha\widehat{r}_{0}}
, which by definition of
\widehat{r}_{0}
and Part (
v
) of Lemma
8
is
O((\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})^{-2\alpha})
, giving

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{rad}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})\;=\;O\big((\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}+\mathfrak{z}(\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha R}\big).

(35)

To obtain a uniform upper bound for
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{rad}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})
on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, observe that for any fixed
r_{0}
, since
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, we have
\theta_{0}\geq\gamma(r_{0})
, where
\gamma
was defined at the beginning of this section, giving

\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0}=\tfrac{1}{3}\theta_{0}-\tfrac{2}{3}\phi(r_{0})\geq\tfrac{1}{3}\gamma(r_{0})+\tfrac{2}{3}\phi(r_{0})\geq\tfrac{1}{3}\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r_{0}}+\phi(r_{0})\geq\tfrac{1}{3}\kappa_{R}e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r_{0}}

(36)

and hence
(\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}=O((\kappa_{R}^{2}e^{(1-2(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2}))r_{0}})^{-\alpha})
.
For the case
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}
this bound is
O(\kappa_{R}^{-2\alpha})
, while for the case
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
the exponential term is equal to
e^{(1-2\beta)r_{0}}
which is at least
e^{(1-2\beta)r^{\prime}}
since as already observed
r_{0}\geq r^{\prime}
. By Part (
ii
) in Fact
42
we have
r^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\beta}\log(\kappa_{R})-O(1)
. Thus,

\kappa_{R}^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha(1-2\beta)r_{0}}\leq\kappa_{R}^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha(1-2\beta)r^{\prime}}=O\big(\kappa_{R}^{-2\alpha}\kappa_{R}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}(1-2\beta)}\big)=O\big(\kappa_{R}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\big)

so putting both cases together we obtain a bound of the form
O(\kappa_{R}^{-\alpha/(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})})
.
Next we address the term
\mathfrak{z}(\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha R}
appearing in (
35
). For
r^{\prime}\leq r_{0}\leq r^{\prime\prime}
, by (
36
), we have

\mathfrak{z}(\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha R}=O\big(\mathfrak{z}\kappa_{R}^{-2\alpha}e^{2\alpha(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r_{0}}e^{-\alpha R}\big)

which is an increasing function of
r_{0}
and hence within
[r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime}]
the bound is maximized at
r^{\prime\prime}
. Thus, it is enough to deal with points with radial component
r_{0}
in
[r^{\prime\prime},R]
. Now, for said points a similar argument to the one in (
36
) gives
\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0}\geq\tfrac{1}{3}\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
and hence

\mathfrak{z}(\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha R}=O\big(\mathfrak{z}\kappa_{A}^{-2\alpha}\big(e^{\frac{R}{2}}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\big)^{-2\alpha}\big).

The analysis of this term depends on
\alpha
and
\beta
, and we begin addressing the case
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
and
\alpha<2\beta
since it is the most delicate. Notice that in this case
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=e^{-\beta R}\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}
so in particular
\mathfrak{z}(e^{\frac{R}{2}}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-2\alpha}=(\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R})^{1-2\beta}
and hence the upper bound is an increasing function of
\mathfrak{z}
. Now, in order for
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
to be non-empty we need
\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\leq\pi
we deduce that
\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}=O(\kappa_{A}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}})
, which gives

\mathfrak{z}\kappa_{A}^{-2\alpha}(e^{\frac{R}{2}}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-2\alpha}=O\big(\kappa_{A}^{-2\alpha}\big(\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}\big)^{1-2\beta}\big)=O\big(\kappa_{A}^{-2\alpha}\kappa_{A}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}(1-2\beta)}\big)=O(\kappa_{A}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}).

The remaining cases are easier: If
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}
, then
e^{\frac{R}{2}}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}
so the term
\mathfrak{z}\kappa_{A}^{-2\alpha}(e^{\frac{R}{2}}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-2\alpha}
is
O(\kappa_{A}^{-2\alpha})
, while for the cases
\alpha=2\beta
and
\alpha>2\beta
it can be easily checked that since
\mathfrak{z}=O(e^{2\beta R})
and
\mathfrak{z}=O(e^{2\beta R}/\log(e^{2\beta R}))
respectively, we obtain
\mathfrak{z}(e^{\frac{R}{2}}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-2\alpha}=o(1)
. Since
\kappa_{R}=\kappa_{A}
whenever
\alpha<2\beta
, putting together the bounds for both terms in (
35
) we finally obtain the sought after bound:

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{rad}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})\;=\;O(\kappa_{R}^{-\alpha/(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})})\;=\;\begin{cases}e^{-\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{2})},&\text{ if $\alpha\geq 2\beta$,}\\[2.0pt]
O(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha/(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})}),&\text{ if $\alpha<2\beta$.}\end{cases}

Next, we show that for
\kappa_{A}=C
we have
\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(T^{rad}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{z})d\mu(x_{0})=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
by integrating both terms in (
35
) over
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
.
For the first one we use that
\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0}\geq\frac{1}{3}\theta_{0}
to obtain

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}(\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}d\mu(x_{0})=O(ne^{-\alpha R})\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\infty}\theta_{0}^{-2\alpha}d\theta_{0}dr_{0}=O(ne^{-\alpha R})\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\frac{1}{\gamma(r_{0})^{2\alpha-1}}dr_{0}.

Splitting the range of integration of the last integral into
[r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime}]
and
[r^{\prime\prime},R]
we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}(\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}d\mu(x_{0})

\displaystyle=O\big(ne^{-\alpha R}\big(e^{(2\alpha-1)(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r^{\prime\prime}}+(R-r^{\prime\prime})(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-(2\alpha-1)}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=O\big(ne^{-\alpha R}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-(2\alpha-1)}\log(e^{(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})R}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})\big)

where the last equality is by definition of
r^{\prime\prime}
and since
e^{(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})R}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=\Omega(1)
implies that
R-r^{\prime\prime}=\Omega(\log(e^{(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})R}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}))
. To address this last bound suppose first that
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
and observe that since
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=\Omega(e^{-\beta R})\cap O(1)
we have

e^{-\alpha R}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-2\alpha}\log(e^{(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})R}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})=O\big(Re^{-2\alpha(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)R}\big)=O(1)
,
while for
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}
we have
\alpha<2\beta
, hence
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=e^{-\frac{R}{2}}\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}
so
e^{-\alpha R}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-2\alpha}\log(e^{(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})R}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})=O\left(\mathfrak{z}^{-1}\log\mathfrak{z}\right)=O(1)
(since
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
) and we conclude that in any case scenario

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}(\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}d\mu(x_{0})=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}).

For the second term in (
35
) we use
\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0}\geq\frac{1}{3}\theta_{0}
again, together with
\gamma(r_{0})\geq C\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathfrak{z}(\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{0})^{-2\alpha}e^{-\alpha R}d\mu(x_{0})

\displaystyle=O(\mathfrak{z}ne^{-\alpha R})\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\int_{C\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}}^{\infty}\theta_{0}^{-2\alpha}d\theta_{0}e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}

\displaystyle=O\big(\mathfrak{z}ne^{-\alpha R}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-(2\alpha-1)}\big),

and it can be checked directly from the definition of
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
and our assumption
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
that this last expression is also
O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
.
∎

The previous proposition gave the uniform bound for the first term appearing in (
33
), as well as a bound for its integral over
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
. The following proposition allows us to bound from above the second term in (
33
) with the use of Proposition
39
to handle the function
\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)
. Recall that in order to apply said proposition we require
\sigma
to be larger than
\sigma_{0}
with

\sigma_{0}:=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}e^{-2\beta R}(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})^{1\vee\frac{2\beta}{\alpha}},&\text{if $\alpha\neq 2\beta$,}\\[2.0pt]
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}e^{-2\beta R}v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z}),&\text{if $\alpha=2\beta$,}\end{cases}

(37)

where
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}
is a large constant that depends on
\alpha
and
\beta
, and where
v_{0}
is some function of
x_{0}
for which we only ask to satisfy
v_{0}>\frac{4}{c}
for
0<c<1
being a constant lower bound for
\mathfrak{z}
(which exists since
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
).

Proposition 44
.

Let
v_{0}:=v_{0}(x_{0})
be a function satisfying
v_{0}>\frac{4}{c}
, and let
\sigma_{0}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{0}
be as in (
32
) and (
37
), respectively. Assume that
\kappa_{A}\geq C
where
C>0
is as in the statement of Proposition
41
and
\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}=\Omega(\sigma_{0})
, then

\int_{0}^{\infty}\tfrac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)d\sigma=O\big(e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}}+e^{-\frac{1}{16}v_{0}^{2}\mathfrak{z}}+\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}+v_{0}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}+\mathfrak{t}_{5}\big),

(38)

where

\mathfrak{t}_{5}=\begin{cases}(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}},&\text{ if $\alpha\neq 2\beta$,}\\[2.0pt]
\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\tfrac{w\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}}}e^{-\frac{w\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}}\left(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})\right)^{w-1}dw,&\text{ if $\alpha=2\beta$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Fix any given
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
and split
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)d\sigma
into

\int_{0}^{\sigma_{0}}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma\big)d\sigma+\int_{\sigma_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)d\sigma.

(39)

For the first integral, we can bound the probability inside it by
1
, which using that
\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}/\sigma_{0}=\Omega(1)

and the change of variables
w:=\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}/\sigma
gives a bound

\int_{0}^{\sigma_{0}}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}d\sigma\,=\,\int_{\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{w}}e^{-\frac{w}{2}}dw\,=\,O\big(e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}}\big)

For the second integral in (
39
), we use (
28
) to bound the probability within together with Proposition
39
(since
\sigma>\sigma_{0}
) and obtain

\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)\,=\,O\big(e^{-\frac{1}{16}v_{0}^{2}\mathfrak{z}}+e^{-\alpha r_{0}}+\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}+v_{0}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}+\big((v_{0}\mathfrak{z})^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}\big)^{1-\mathfrak{e}}\big)

(40)

where
\mathfrak{e}:=\frac{1}{\sigma e^{2\beta R}}\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})=\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma}
if
\alpha=2\beta
, and
\mathfrak{e}=0
otherwise. Grouping the first, second and fourth terms in (
40
), the assumption
\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}/\sigma_{0}=\Omega(1)
gives

\int_{\sigma_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\big(e^{-\frac{1}{16}v_{0}^{2}\mathfrak{z}}+e^{-\alpha r_{0}}+v_{0}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}\big)d\sigma=O\big(e^{-\frac{1}{16}v_{0}^{2}\mathfrak{z}}+e^{-\alpha r_{0}}+v_{0}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}\big)

since the terms are independent of
\sigma
and
\int_{\sigma_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}d\sigma=\int_{0}^{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}/\sigma_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{w}}e^{-\frac{w}{2}}dw=O(1)
. For the term
\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}
analogous computations give

\int_{\sigma_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}d\sigma\,=\,e^{-\alpha r_{0}}\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\int_{0}^{\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}}}w^{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{w}{2}}dw\,=\,O\big(e^{-\alpha r_{0}}\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\big).

The final term in (
40
) is a bit more delicate, and must be treated differently according to whether
\alpha=2\beta
or
\alpha\neq 2\beta
. In the latter case we can repeat the computations used in the previous term, giving

\int_{\sigma_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}d\sigma=O\big((v_{0}\mathfrak{z})^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\big),

whereas if
\alpha=2\beta
, we use the change of variables
w:=\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma}
and the fact that
v_{0}\mathfrak{z}\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}=\sigma_{0}/(\sigma\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z}))=w/(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z}))
so that

\displaystyle\int_{\sigma_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\big((v_{0}\mathfrak{z})^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}\big)^{1-\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma}}d\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sqrt{\sigma_{0}w}}e^{-\frac{w\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}}\Big(\frac{w}{\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})}\Big)^{1-w}dw

\displaystyle=O\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\tfrac{w\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}}}e^{-\frac{w\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}}\Big(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})\Big)^{w-1}dw\Big).

The result then follows by adding all the bounds and noticing that
e^{-\alpha r_{0}}=O(\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha r_{0}})
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Proposition
41
.

Using (
33
) and Proposition
43
it will be enough to obtain a uniform bound for
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)d\sigma
on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
as well as an upper bound for the integral of this term over this set. We will address the uniform bounds first, since calculations are easier, and then turn to the upper bound for the integrals over
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, whose analysis is similar.

Uniform upper bound:
Our goal is to show that under the hypothesis
\mathfrak{z}=\omega(1)
,

\sup_{x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\tfrac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma)d\sigma=\begin{cases}e^{-\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{2})},&\text{ if $\alpha\geq 2\beta$,}\\[2.0pt]
O(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha/(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})}),&\text{ if $\alpha<2\beta$}.\end{cases}

(41)

Throughout this analysis, we take
v_{0}
constant and equal to
\frac{4}{c}
. With this choice of
v_{0}
we deduce from Part (
iv
) in Fact
42
that
\sigma_{0}=\Theta((\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{2\vee 4\beta})=O((\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0}))^{2})=O(\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2})
and hence we have (
38
) as in Proposition
44
, so we address each term appearing in the bound as follows:

We assume throughout the uniform upper bound proof that
x_{0}=(r_{0},\theta_{0})\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, and hence this set is non empty so in particular we must have
\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}+\phi(R)\leq\pi
.

•

For the term
e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}}
it follows from the definition of
\widehat{\theta}_{0}
that it is equal to
e^{-\frac{2}{9\sigma_{0}}(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))^{2}}
and since
|\theta_{0}|\geq\gamma(r_{0})
by Part (
iv
) in Fact
42
we deduce
(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))^{2}=\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{2}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{2})
.
Recalling that
\sigma_{0}=\Theta((\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{2\vee 4\beta})
the term
e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}}
equals
e^{-\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{2})}
, which is at most of the same order than the one claimed in (
41
).

•

The term
e^{-\frac{1}{16}v_{0}^{2}\mathfrak{z}}
appearing in (
38
) does not depend on
x_{0}
and since we are assuming
\mathfrak{z}=\omega(1)
, it becomes
o(1)
, so it is negligible.

•

Now, we consider the term
\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}
. By definition
\widehat{\theta}_{0}=\Theta(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))
.
Since
|\theta_{0}|\geq\gamma(r_{0})\geq\phi(r_{0})
for
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, by
Part (
iv
) in Fact
42
, we have

\big((|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}=O\big(\kappa_{R}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{\alpha((1\wedge\frac{1}{2\beta})-1)r_{0}}\big),

If
\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}
, this bound is
O(\kappa_{R}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}})
independently of
r_{0}
, whilst if
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
, using that
r_{0}\geq r^{\prime}
, the bound is
O(\kappa_{R}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}/e^{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}(2\beta-1)r_{0}})=O(\kappa_{R}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}/e^{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}(2\beta-1)r^{\prime}})
. Recalling that, by Part (
ii
) in Fact
42
, we know that
r^{\prime}=2\log\kappa_{R}-O(1)
, replacing this value in the previous bound we obtain a term of order
\kappa_{R}^{-2\alpha}
. Summarizing, we have shown that
\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}=O(\kappa_{R}^{-\alpha(2\vee\frac{1}{\beta})})
and the result then follows by our choice of
\kappa_{R}
.

•

For the term
v_{0}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}
we observe that it is independent of
x_{0}
, and under the assumptions
\mathfrak{z}=O(e^{\alpha R}/R)
if
\alpha=2\beta
and
\mathfrak{z}=O(e^{2\beta R})
if
\alpha>2\beta
in the statement of Proposition
37
we obtain that
\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}=o(1)
and hence the term is negligible. In the case
\alpha<2\beta
, since
\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}+\phi(R)\leq\pi
we obtain
\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}=O(\kappa_{A}^{-\alpha/(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})})
by definition of
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
.

•

To address the term
\mathfrak{t}_{5}
we first treat the case
\alpha\neq 2\beta
. Using the definition of
\widehat{\theta}_{0}
and the fact that
\theta_{0}\geq\gamma(r_{0})\geq\phi(r_{0})
this term is of order

\mathfrak{z}^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}=O\big(\mathfrak{z}^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0}))^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}\big)

and using Part (
iv
) in Fact
42
we deduce

\mathfrak{z}^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}=O\big(\mathfrak{z}^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}(\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}\big)=O((\kappa_{A}\mathfrak{z}^{-(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\vee\frac{1}{4})}e^{\beta R}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}).

(42)

From the definition of
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
we have

\mathfrak{z}^{-(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\vee\frac{1}{4})}e^{\beta R}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=\begin{cases}(e^{\frac{R}{2}}\mathfrak{z}^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}})^{0\vee(2\beta-1)},&\text{ if $\alpha<2\beta$,}\\[2.0pt]
\mathfrak{z}^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{\alpha})\vee\frac{1}{4}},&\text{ if $\alpha>2\beta$,}\end{cases}

where we directly observe that if
\alpha>2\beta
this expression is
\omega(1)
(since
\mathfrak{z}=\omega(1)
), and hence the upper bound is
o(1)
so in particular it has the form
e^{-\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{2})}
as desired. For the case
\alpha<2\beta
we distinguish between the cases
\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
: In the former we directly obtain that
\mathfrak{z}^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}(\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha R}=O(\kappa_{A}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}})
, whereas if
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
, since
\phi(R)+\kappa_{A}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\leq\pi
and by definition of
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
, we have

\mathfrak{z}^{-(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\vee\frac{1}{4})}e^{\beta R}\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=(1/\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{2\beta-1}=\Omega(\kappa_{A}^{-(2\beta-1)})
and obtain an upper bound of
O\big(\kappa_{A}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\kappa_{A}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}(2\beta-1)}\big)=O(\kappa_{A}^{-2\alpha})
.

Assume now that
\alpha=2\beta
so that
\mathfrak{t}_{5}=\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\tfrac{w\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}}}e^{-\frac{w\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}}\left(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})\right)^{w-1}dw
and notice that since the function
x\to xe^{-x^{2}}
is
O(1)
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and using that
\mathfrak{z}=\omega(1)
we obtain

\mathfrak{t}_{5}=O\Big(\int_{0}^{1}(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z}))^{w-1}dw\Big)=o(1)

and hence the term is negligible in this case.

Integral upper bound:
Our goal is to show that under the hypothesis
\kappa_{A}=C
for
C
large and
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
,

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\tfrac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}}{\sigma^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}\big(I(\mathfrak{z})\geq\sigma\big)d\sigma dx_{0}=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}).

In contrast to the choice in the uniform upper bound analysis, we will now choose

v_{0}(x_{0})\;:=\;\frac{4}{c}\Big(\frac{|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0})}{\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0})}\Big)^{\varepsilon}

for some fixed
\varepsilon>0
satisfying
\varepsilon<(2\alpha-1)(1\wedge\frac{\alpha}{2\beta})
(this is possible since
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
) and where
0<c<1
is a lower bound for
\mathfrak{z}
. It will be convenient to define
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon/(1\wedge\frac{\alpha}{2\beta})<1
since it will appear many times in subsequent computations. In order to apply Proposition
44
we need to show that
\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}=\Omega(\sigma_{0})
, which is less straightforward than the uniform bound case since
\sigma_{0}
is also increasing with
\theta_{0}
. In the case
\alpha\neq 2\beta
, observe that
|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0})=\Omega(v_{0}^{1/\varepsilon}(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0})))
while at the same time
\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0})=\Omega(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})=\Omega(e^{-\beta R}\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{2}\vee\frac{\beta}{\alpha}})=\Omega(v_{0}^{-\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{2\varepsilon}}\sqrt{\sigma_{0}})
. We conclude that

\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}}=\frac{4}{9\sigma_{0}}(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))^{2}=\Omega(v_{0}^{\frac{2-\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}}),

(43)

which is
\Omega(1)
since
v_{0}=\Omega(1)
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}<2
. Analogously, if
\alpha=2\beta
we still have
|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0})=\Omega(v_{0}^{1/\varepsilon}(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0})))
whereas this time
\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0})=\Omega(\sqrt{\frac{\sigma_{0}\log\mathfrak{z}}{v_{0}\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})}})
so that

\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}}=\frac{4}{9\sigma_{0}}(|\theta_{0}|-\phi(r_{0}))^{2}=\Omega(v_{0}^{\frac{2-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}}\tfrac{\log\mathfrak{z}}{\log(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})}),

(44)

which is again
\Omega(1)
since both
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
and
v_{0}=\Omega(1)
. We have proved that in either case
\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}=\Omega(\sigma_{0})
. As a result we have (
38
) where we address each term appearing in the upper bound as follows:

•

For the term
e^{-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}}
assume first that
\alpha\neq 2\beta
and use (
43
) to obtain

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\exp\Big({-}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}\Big)d\mu(x_{0})=n\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\infty}\exp\Big({-}\Omega\big(v_{0}^{\frac{2-\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{0})\big)\Big)d\theta_{0}e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0},

so using the change of variable
w:=\frac{\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0})}{\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0})}
we obtain

\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\infty}\exp\Big({-}\Omega\big(v_{0}^{\frac{2-\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{0})\big)\Big)d\theta_{0}=(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0}))\int_{1}^{\infty}e^{-\Omega(w^{2-\varepsilon^{\prime}})}dw=O(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0})),

and hence

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\exp\Big(-\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}\Big)d\mu(x_{0})=O\Big(n\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0}))e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}\Big)=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}),

where the last equality follows from Part (
v
) in Fact
42
.

For the case
\alpha=2\beta
we follow the same reasoning using (
44
) to deduce

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\exp\Big({-}\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}}\Big)d\mu(x_{0})

\displaystyle=n\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\Omega\left(v_{0}^{\frac{2-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}}(\theta_{0})\tfrac{\log(\mathfrak{z})}{\log(v_{0}(\theta_{0})\mathfrak{z})}\right)\right)d\theta_{0}e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}

\displaystyle=n\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0}))\int_{1}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\Omega(w^{2-\varepsilon^{\prime}}\tfrac{\log(\mathfrak{z})}{\log(w^{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{z})})\right)dwe^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0},

which is
O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
as before.

•

For the term
e^{-\frac{1}{16}v_{0}^{2}\mathfrak{z}}
appearing in (
38
) we use the change of variable
w=(v_{0}\sqrt{\mathfrak{z}})^{1/\varepsilon}
so that

\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{1}{16}v_{0}^{2}\mathfrak{z}}d\theta_{0}=\mathfrak{z}^{-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}}(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0}))\int_{\Omega(\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}})}^{\infty}e^{-\Omega(w^{2\varepsilon})}dw=O(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0})),

where the last equality follows from
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
. Hence,

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}e^{-\frac{1}{16}v_{0}^{2}\mathfrak{z}}d\mu(x_{0})=O\Big(n\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0}))e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}\Big)=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}),

where the last equality follows from Part (
v
) in Fact
42
.

•

For the term
\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}
, by definition of
\widehat{\theta}_{0}
we obtain

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}e^{-\alpha r_{0}}d\mu(x_{0})\,=\,O\Big(ne^{-\alpha R}\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\pi}(\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0}))^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}d\theta_{0}dr_{0}\Big).

We treat first the case
\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}
, which implies in particular
\frac{\alpha}{\beta}>1
and hence the right-hand side is of order

ne^{-\alpha R}\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0}))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}dr_{0}\,=\,O\big(ne^{-\alpha R}(e^{-(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})r^{\prime\prime}})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}+ne^{-\alpha R}(R-r^{\prime\prime})(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\big)

where we first integrate from
r^{\prime}
to
r^{\prime\prime}
, and then from
r^{\prime\prime}
to
R
, and then used Part (
iv
) in Fact
42
to bound
\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0})
from below. Using Part (
iii
) of Fact
42
in the first term on the right-hand side, we obtain a term of order
ne^{-\alpha R}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
, while for the second term using that
\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}
we have by definition that
r^{\prime\prime}=-\frac{1}{\beta}\log\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}+\Theta(1)=R-\Theta(\log\mathfrak{z})
so in particular
ne^{-\alpha R}(R-r^{\prime\prime})(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\mathfrak{z}^{-\delta}\log\mathfrak{z})=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
for some
\delta>0
depending on
\alpha
and
\beta
, giving the result in this case.
For the case
\alpha>\beta>\frac{1}{2}
we can repeat the calculations in the previous case to again obtain a bound of order
n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
.

For the case
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
we have
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}R}\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}
and we still need to handle the case
\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\leq 1
. If
\frac{\alpha}{\beta}<1
, we have

ne^{-\alpha R}\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\pi}(\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0}))^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}d\theta_{0}dr_{0}\,=\,O\big(ne^{-\alpha R}R\big),

and in it is easily checked that since
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and by the definition of
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
we have
ne^{-\alpha R}R=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}e^{(\frac{1}{2}-\alpha)R}R)=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
. Finally, if
\frac{\alpha}{\beta}=1
we have

ne^{-\alpha R}\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\pi}(\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0}))^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}d\theta_{0}dr_{0}\,=\,O\Big(ne^{-\alpha R}\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\log\big(\tfrac{\pi}{\gamma(r_{0}){-}\phi(r_{0})}\big)dr_{0}\Big)

where using Part (
i
) in Fact
42
we conclude that the right-hand term is at most of order
ne^{-\alpha R}R\log(1/\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})=O(ne^{-\alpha R}R^{2})=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
.

•

For the term
v_{0}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}
we observe that, using the change of variable

w:=v_{0}^{1/\varepsilon}
together with Part (
iv
) in Fact
42
, we get

\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\pi}v_{0}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}d\theta_{0}=O(\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0}))^{-\varepsilon})\int_{0}^{\pi}wdw=O(\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\varepsilon}).

Now, suppose first that
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
so that
e^{-\alpha R}=\mathfrak{z}^{-1}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{2\alpha}
and hence
\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\varepsilon}=(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{2\alpha-\varepsilon}=O(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
where the last equality
is by our assumption of
\varepsilon<2\alpha-1
. Suppose now that
\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}
and observe that in this case
e^{-\alpha R}=O((\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\mathfrak{z}^{-(1\vee\frac{\alpha}{2\beta})})

and
\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}\leq\alpha<1
so using that
\mathfrak{z}=\Omega(1)
we deduce again that

\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\varepsilon}=O\left((\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{2\alpha-\varepsilon}\right)=O(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
.
Summarizing, independent of the value taken by
\beta
we have

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}v_{0}\mathfrak{z}e^{-\alpha R}d\mu(x_{0})=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}=O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}).

•

For the term
\mathfrak{t}_{5}
it will be useful to notice from Part
iv
in Fact
42
that

\left(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0})\right)^{-\delta}=O((\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\delta})

(45)

for any
\delta>0
. We assume first that
\alpha\neq 2\beta
so that

\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\mathfrak{t}_{5}(x_{0})d\mu(x_{0})\,=\,O\Big(ne^{-\alpha R}\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\pi}(v_{0}\mathfrak{z})^{1\vee\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}d\theta_{0}e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}\Big)

(46)

We analyze this expression first when
\alpha\leq 4\beta
so using (
45
) the term on the right-hand side is of order

ne^{-\alpha R}\mathfrak{z}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\varepsilon}\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\pi}(\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0}))^{\varepsilon-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}d\theta_{0}e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0},

(47)

where the integral with respect to
\theta_{0}
behaves differently according to whether
\varepsilon-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}
is smaller, equal, or larger than
{-}1
. Suppose first that
\varepsilon-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}<{-}1
so that the above term is of order

ne^{-\alpha R}\mathfrak{z}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\varepsilon}\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}(\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0}))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}+\varepsilon}e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}\,=\,O\big(ne^{-\alpha R}\mathfrak{z}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\big),

where the right hand side follows from (
45
). Proceeding as in (
42
) we deduce that the bound is
O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
. Suppose now that
\varepsilon-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\geq-1
, which in particular implies
\beta\geq\frac{\alpha}{1+\varepsilon}\geq\frac{\alpha}{1+(2\alpha-1)}=\frac{1}{2}
so in this case
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}R}\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}
. Now, if
\varepsilon-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}>-1
then the integral with respect to
\theta_{0}
in (
47
) is
O(1)
, and thus the bound is of order

ne^{-\alpha R}\mathfrak{z}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\varepsilon}\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}=O\big(ne^{-\alpha R}\mathfrak{z}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\varepsilon}\big)=O\big(n(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{2\alpha-\varepsilon}\big)

where we have used the particular form of
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}
. Since
\varepsilon
satisfies
\varepsilon<2\alpha-1
and also
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}\leq\pi=O(1)
, we conclude that the bound is
O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
. Similarly, if
\varepsilon-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}=-1
then (
47
) is of order

ne^{-\alpha R}\mathfrak{z}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\varepsilon}\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\gamma(r_{0})-\phi(r_{0})}\big)e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}=O\big(ne^{-\alpha R}\mathfrak{z}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\varepsilon}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}}\big)\big),

which is of order
n(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{2\alpha-\varepsilon}\log(\frac{1}{\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}})
and again using that
\varepsilon<2\alpha-1
and
\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})}=O(1)
we conclude that the bound is
O(n\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})
.

We now turn to the analysis of the right-hand side of (
46
) when
\alpha>4\beta
, in which case we obtain a term of order

ne^{-\alpha R}\mathfrak{z}^{\frac{\alpha}{4\beta}}(\phi^{(\mathfrak{z})})^{-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{4\beta}}\int_{r^{\prime}}^{R}\int_{\gamma(r_{0})}^{\pi}(\theta_{0}-\phi(r_{0}))^{\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{4\beta}-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}d\theta_{0}e^{-\alpha(R-r_{0})}dr_{0}.

(48)

Observe that the condition
\alpha>4\beta
implies that
